A tape whose exchange door, request (via a changed random word), output (via a changed reply), record kind (Mono and Clock swapped) or length is wrong is refused with DoorDiverged, RequestDiverged, OutputDiverged, KindMismatch or Exhausted at the expected position, an output past the end of the tape included (Exhausted at that output, not a pass), and the refusal consumes nothing; a tape longer than the run -- a last output the replay never says, or a Raised record whose rise it never polled up to, which no door sees -- replays every door it reaches and is refused where the program ends by ReplaySeam::finish with Unreached naming the first record left, consuming nothing, while the exact tape finishes clean; a Raised record ahead of an exchange is refused, not skipped, and so is one a replay reaches after fewer polls than its false-read count. Polls answering [F,F,T,T] tape one record and replay the same answers; false-read counts are per door and restart at every record; a flag that rises and falls reads latched on the recording, live and replay seams alike. The untaped LiveSeam answers what RecordingSeam answers over the same world; RecordingSeam::with_sink streams the same records, and ReplaySeam::streaming pulls at most one record ahead of the program. Every refusal's Display and Debug stay under a KiB when its own bytes, or the record it found, carry a 64 KiB screen, and a diverged request or output names the first byte where the two differ. In-memory and caller-sink tapes only: no framing or durability is claimed. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-effects --test suite -- exchange_replay::` |
| `effects.keyed-view` | astream-effects::KeyedView offers read-only lookup of every Exchange of a record slice by (door, request); lookup(door, request, seen) returns the latest matching exchange among the first `seen` records, with its position, never a later one; `seen` is a count of records read, so a reader beside a replay passes the replay's position() and is never answered from the record that replay is about to read, and 0 (nothing read) knows nothing. A miss -- never asked, asked only later, the same request through another door, another request through the same door, or an Emit or Raised record carrying the same bytes -- is ReplayDivergence::Unrecorded. Fifteen fixed lookups over a nine-record tape give the same answers asked forwards, backwards, and each twice in a row, so a lookup never consumes and does not depend on the order lookups are asked in; a view built over a recorded demo tape finds its exchange, and an in-order replay of the same records still reproduces the recorded digest; stepped beside a replay, a lookup at its position() misses the exchange until the replay has read it, and at every bound from 0 to the tape's length every hit lies before the bound. The view holds only borrowed records, so it has no live world to call. KeyedIndex is the same index over records it holds (a Vec, a Box or an Arc of them), answering through the same search: it gives every fixed lookup's answer, forwards, backwards and twice, over each of the three; one index over an Arc is Send + Sync + 'static and serves four threads the same answers; and over a deterministic 1,500-record tape that holds all eight record kinds (checked; a file read or a failed one names an exchange's door as its path, and a file read carries that exchange's request as its bytes, so an index that took a file read for an exchange answers wrongly there), 48 (door, request) keys (some never asked) at every bound from 0 to past the end are answered by the view and by the index exactly as a backwards scan of the records read answers them, while a scan that admits the record at the bound disagrees somewhere on that tape. Eager index construction is limited to 1,048,576 records; a boundary regression requires an indexed tape at that limit, then allocation-free lookup beyond it, preserving exact exchange counts, the latest answer before each cutoff (including the last indexed record and one beyond the budget), repeated and reversed queries, typed misses, and exclusion of an output record carrying the requested bytes. aterm calls this crate directly: aterm-agent's harness tapes (supervise::tape) are EffectRecords recorded through RecordingSeam and replayed through ReplaySeam, its tape cursor admits a recorded exchange by keyed_hit_within_cutoff, the very cutoff KeyedView::lookup and KeyedIndex::lookup admit by, its policy probe (supervise::policy::probe) shares one KeyedIndex over an Arc of a tape's records, and its tests hold its own lookups to KeyedView; agh's every clock, sleep and GitHub round trip crosses this crate's seam (agh::fx: LiveSeam over OsEffects, RecordingSeam, ReplaySeam), so a run is taped and replayed offline. aterm frames and encodes those tapes itself, so what it relies on here is EffectRecord's shape, the replay's divergences, and the keyed cutoff: a hit strictly before `seen`, never at it. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-effects --lib -- keyed::tests::eager_index_budget_preserves_every_lookup && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-effects --test suite -- keyed_view::` |
| `effects.foreign-process.record-replay` | On Linux, astream-host::foreign forks a child (no exec) running a fixed effect program -- a wall clock, eight random bytes, a file read, folded order-sensitively -- through raw syscalls that a ptrace tracer intercepts. The child never sees taped values but is astream-authored (it self-attaches with PTRACE_TRACEME), not an unmodified binary. Record tapes each result into the astream-effects EffectsLog; replay overwrites each result (return register and output buffer), reproducing the recorded digest in a deliberately changed world where a no-inject replay diverges. The digest matches an independent oracle; a short, wrong-kind, path-divergent (checked against the requested path, not the tracee's openat argument), or over-buffer tape aborts. The kernel still executes each replayed syscall. Linux-gated: astream's only gate is `make ci` on macOS, which reports this claim skipped, never verified, and nothing runs it on Linux. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-host --test effects_foreign_process` (re-run on linux only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `effects.seccomp-bpf-filter` | astream-host::seccomp (`Astream.Host.TlaSeccompFilter`, zero unsafe) compiles the 11-instruction DAG-bounded classic-BPF `sock_filter` program (`compile_five_syscall_filter`) for both `AUDIT_ARCH_X86_64` and `AUDIT_ARCH_AARCH64`, verified across syscalls 0..512 by `eval_cbpf_seccomp`: foreign `seccomp_data.arch` values return `SECCOMP_RET_KILL_PROCESS`, `{clock_gettime, getrandom, openat, read, close}` return `SECCOMP_RET_TRACE`, and every other syscall returns `SECCOMP_RET_ALLOW` (`O(intercepted)` tracer stops). `NeutralizedReplayDriver` drives replay-time `-1` syscall neutralization (`NEUTRALIZED_SYSCALL_NR`), loader `openat`/`read`/`close` passthrough, `SYNTHETIC_REPLAY_FD` target-file virtualization, and fail-closed `ReplayAbort` on short, wrong-kind, path-divergent, and over-buffer tapes. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-host --lib seccomp:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-host --test seccomp_record_replay -- bpf_and_neutralization::` |
| `effects.foreign-process.seccomp-record-replay` | On Linux (`x86_64` and `aarch64`, cross-compiled under `make stock`), `astream-host::foreign::{record_exec, replay_exec}` forks, attaches `PTRACE_O_TRACESECCOMP`, installs `seccomp::compile_five_syscall_filter` via `seccomp(SECCOMP_SET_MODE_FILTER)`, and `execve`s the standalone unmodified `seccomp_child` binary (`#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`, zero `PTRACE_TRACEME`). Record tapes `[Clock, Rand, File]` in `O(intercepted)` stops while passing dynamic-linker I/O through; replay rewrites every intercepted target syscall to `-1` (`orig_rax` / `NT_ARM_SYSTEM_CALL`) before kernel execution and injects taped returns and buffers, reproducing the independent oracle digest even after the target file is deleted from disk, and aborting fail-closed on short, wrong-kind, path-divergent, or over-buffer tapes. Linux-gated: `make ci` on macOS verifies the cBPF filter and neutralization driver under `effects.seccomp-bpf-filter` and cross-compiles the Linux tracer under `make stock`, while reporting this kernel-execution claim skipped on macOS. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-host --test seccomp_record_replay` (re-run on linux only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `term.cognition.replay-and-fork` | astream-agent records a bounded agent turn as a separate cognition log (CogRecord::Completion and ToolResult envelopes in CRC frames) and replays it without network or tools: a fixed hand-authored 5-record transcript drives a planner loop whose ReplayPlanner is a deterministic content-keyed stand-in for model cognition, emitting a decision trace. Two replays are bit-identical; swapping one recorded ToolResult (BUILD FAILED -> BUILD OK) with cog_fork_swap re-drives the same fixed policy down another branch, so the alternate trace shares a prefix then diverges and the alternate screen omits the recovery and shows BUILD OK. Replay re-feeds recorded completion bytes to a fixed policy; it does not recompute the model and makes no model-fidelity claim. Live capture (astream-live, #[ignore]-gated) is not exercised. | `targo --unverified run --quiet --locked -p astream-agent --example agent_replay` |
| `term.fleet.durable-watermark` | A child's injected In durably stamps the orchestrator Out that caused it in its envelope header (a caused_by (Partition, Offset)). After the live sessions are dropped, cross_edges_from_logs decodes the stored child-log bytes alone and rebuilds a CrossEdge equal field-for-field to one built from the offsets the appends returned; with no cause stamped there is no edge, and a different cause offset yields a different edge set. is_consistent over the rebuilt edge rejects the orphan cut and admits the in-flight cut, and replay_to_cut re-folds the child screen byte-equal to a direct prefix fold. materialize_fleet_cut builds a collective state-verb FleetSnapshot across partitions over a verified consistent cut (refusing an orphaned-effect cut with FleetError::InconsistentCut), carrying a deterministic collective_hash, and resume_fleet_cut folds only the post-cut tails to match a fresh full-log fold byte-identically. Over a child log whose writer restarted at seq 0, the scan emits only the edge in the intact prefix and recover reports an out-of-order fault. It does not prove a pointer names the record that truly produced the effect (honest pointers are assumed), nor recover unstamped injections. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-engine --test suite -- fleet_durable_watermark::` |
| `term.echo.caused-by-retire` | When echoes reorder, positional/watermark retirement (Predictor::apply_output_at) mis-assigns a verdict and exact caused_by retirement (apply_output_caused) does not. The fixture log holds two real In records applied through session ingest and their echoes arriving reordered, each Out's decoded caused_by naming an earlier In; the engine refuses a same-log cause not strictly earlier than its record (nothing is written). caused_by retirement matches each Out to its keystroke by in-offset and decides the verdict from the Out's bytes, agreeing with an independently built ground truth, while the positional foil marks keystroke A Contradicted. authoritative() still equals the pure fold of the outs in arrival order. The correlation is authored by the fixture (pointers are stamped, not observed from PTY I/O), and caused_by fixes verdict assignment, not the screen's order-sensitivity. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-engine --test suite -- caused_by_retire::` |
| `term.fold.bce` | The fold honours background-colour-erase: ED and EL, a line scrolled in (full screen or inside a DECSTBM region) and the alternate-screen clears fill with the current SGR pen background, while a default erase stays a default blank and cells outside the erase are untouched, asserted against Color::Indexed/Color::Default literals. The typed screen-op projection stays lossless through bce with no new op (emit_ops then apply_ops reproduces the background byte-equal). Cross-validation against the aterm engine is aterm's differential VT oracle, outside this tree and not run by this command; reverse-video bce is out of scope. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-term --test suite -- bce::` |
| `term.fold.underline` | The fold keeps the underline's style and colour on every cell and on the pen (Cell::underline, Cell::ul and Cell::ul_rgb), as kitty, VTE and aterm keep them: 4 is a single underline and 21 a double one, 4:0 to 4:5 name none, single, double, curly, dotted and dashed, 4:n past 5 is a single one (aterm's choice) and what follows n is nothing, and 24 takes it off; 58 sets the colour in each spelling 38 takes (58;5;n, 58;2;r;g;b, the colon groups with an empty colour-space id, with one and with none, on the code or on the selector, a channel past 255 clamped), a palette colour and a 24-bit one replace each other, a spelling that names nothing leaves it, and 59 takes it off while 24 and 4:0 leave it; SGR 0, spelled out or empty, resets both; a glyph takes both from the pen whether or not it is underlined, a wide glyph's spacer as its lead, DECSC saves them and DECRC of a slot never saved resets them, and a bce blank carries neither. Each answer is written out by hand and holds fed whole and a byte at a time; the negative controls are a fold that drops 58's colour, which fails every answer that names a colour, and a fold that knows one underline style, which fails every answer with another. serialize() tells every style and colour apart, a caller's Cell whose attrs hold the encoding's underline bit (WIRE_UNDERLINE, bit 1, where the fold kept its one underline bit) included, while a screen whose underlines are single and uncoloured writes no trailer for them and keeps its bytes. The typed screen-op projection stays lossless (SetSgr's bit 1 says the pen underlines; SetUnderline, tag 0x22, carries a style past single or a colour) over 400 dependency-free random programs of underline SGRs in every spelling mixed with text, wide glyphs, erases, DECSC/DECRC and the alternate screen, through the wire codec, which reach every style and both kinds of colour on a cell, and a DECSC slot that saved a style past single and one that saved a colour. Cross-validated against the production aterm engine, style and colour cell by cell, by aterm's differential VT oracle, which this command does not run. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-term --test suite -- underline::` |
| `term.session.unified-replay-and-fork` | A session carrying all four streams -- Inputs, Outputs, Effects (clock/rng/file), Cognition (Completion/ToolResult) -- on one offset axis replays deterministically (decision trace, applied-input count, screen and its content hash, and effect digest each reconstruct bit-identically twice), and a counterfactual fork of one cognition record makes all four diverge coherently: swapping the failed build result for a clean one ends the turn early, so the trace is a shorter shared prefix, the screen omits the recovery output and its hash moves, fewer inputs apply, and the effect digest differs. Multi-session fleets (`astream-agent::fleet`) replay across Chandy-Lamport consistent cuts (`replay_fleet_cut`, refusing `InconsistentCut` before replaying) and propagate counterfactual forks across event-level causal DAGs (`fork_fleet_session` + `replay_fleet_causal`, resolving multi-round orchestrator <-> worker <-> sub-worker edges, causally pruning downstream sessions when an upstream fork finishes before their spawning offset while preserving `effective_cut` Chandy-Lamport consistency, and rejecting `CausalCycle`s). The effect digest is kind- and path-aware (swapping Clock for Rand, a read's path, or an empty for a failed read moves it). This replays and forks recorded sessions; live capture (astream-live, #[ignore]-gated) is not exercised, and the screen hash is a proxy, not pixel-exact rendering. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-agent --test suite -- unified::` |
| `cognition.live-bridge.parse-and-replay` | The live-capture bridge (no third-party deps; a hand-rolled recursive-descent JSON parser) turns a real-format Anthropic Messages response -- text and tool_use blocks with nested input, plus stop_reason -- into a CogRecord: nested input is re-serialized to round-trippable bytes (control characters escaped, integers beyond 2^53 kept verbatim, non-RFC-8259 number lexemes rejected), and malformed input, including over-deep nesting and ill-formed surrogate escapes, is rejected without panicking. unified_from_capture assembles the four streams from the parsed completion plus a caller-supplied tool output, error flag and clock value (the bridge does not run the tool), and the result replays bit-identically through astream_agent::replay_session and forks coherently. The keyed API call and the Claude CLI call are #[ignore] tests, not exercised here. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-live --test suite -- capture_replays::` |
| `engine.durable.exactly-once-resume` | astream-engine::WorkflowJournal and astream-engine::dag::{DagSpec, DagJournal} commit each step's output to a Strict (fsync-per-append) CRC-framed journal (linear step records and `ADAG` v1 DAG records carrying per-node SHA-256 dependency-set digests). DagSpec validates acyclicity via Kahn's lexicographic topological sort and rejects self-loops, 3-cycles, unknown dependencies, and duplicate names; DagJournal executes ready steps in deterministic topological order with upstream dependency outputs passed as inputs, refuses topological violations or spec drift (DagJournalFault::SpecMismatch) fail-closed without re-running committed steps, and repairs via open_truncating. Across every inter-step boundary and every pair of crashes on linear workflows (1..=3 steps) and diamond/fan-in DAGs (5 nodes), each step runs at most once per durable commit and outputs equal an uninterrupted run. Single-writer and in-process only. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-engine --test suite -- durable::` |
| `engine.merkle-dag` | astream-engine::merkle::{CommitHash, MerkleNode, MerkleChain, lca, diff_chains, merge_input_branches} content-addresses Envelope session histories (`AMRK\x01` domain-separated SHA-256 over parent commit hashes, offset, and canonical ENV_VERSION=3 envelope bytes) under a Clean-Origin (`merkle_parent_count_clamp`) + TLA+ (`Astream.Engine.TlaMerkleDag`) kernel. MerkleChain::from_log_bytes round-trips stored session logs and MerkleChain::verify catches single-bit payload tampering and offset discontinuities; lca and diff_chains isolate shared prefixes and divergent suffixes across counterfactual branches in O(len); merge_input_branches symmetrically merges pure Record::In branches sharing an LCA (deduplicating idempotent `(producer_id, seq)` retries and sorting deterministically) while refusing Record::Out / Record::Resize / Record::Exit suffixes or same-key payload equivocation. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-engine --test suite -- merkle_dag::` |
| `broker.exactly-once-pubsub-resume` | astream-broker, a thread-per-connection daemon (here over a Unix-domain socket), is exercised over real sockets with an in-process broker and no sleeps: (1) publish is exactly-once ingest -- a re-sent (producer_id, producer_seq) is not re-appended and returns its original offset from a Strict durable log; (2) Subscribe(filter, from_offset) streams matching records in dense offset order, then tails new ones live; (3) a consumer that drops and reconnects at last_committed+1 resumes with no gap or duplicate; (4) replay from 0 is byte-identical twice; (5) non-matching subjects are excluded; (6) acked publishes and the dedup map survive a restart; (7) an unserved Broker releases the log lock, serve never displaces a live broker, and when multiple listeners share one Broker, dropping or shutting down one listener handle keeps the remaining listeners and the shared writer alive until the last handle stops. Delivery is at-least-once; exactly-once needs an offset-cursor consumer (see broker.true-exactly-once-e2e). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- exactly_once_pubsub_resume:: lifecycle::` |
| `broker.blocking-accept` | The Unix and TCP acceptors block in accept() instead of polling, so no new connection waits out an acceptor sleep and an idle broker's acceptors never wake; counted, not timed, through BrokerStats (accepted, acceptor_wakeups, acceptor_sleeps, acceptors_abandoned) and BrokerHandle::blocks_in_accept. Over real sockets: 32 back-to-back connect+Hello round trips on Unix, plain TCP and sealed TCP, and a 128-connection burst from 8 threads, each count exactly one acceptor wakeup per connection and zero sleeps; two idle listeners count zero wakeups over a 300 ms window. Shutdown dials the listener until the acceptor's exit signal fires and returns in every embedder pattern: the socket path unlinked by the embedder first (reached through `<socket>.wake`, a broker-owned hard link to the same inode, removed at shutdown), TCP, sealed TCP, and a second broker bound on the unlinked path (it finds the first one's wake name live, polls instead, and the first one's shutdown leaves its socket alone). A stale socket at the wake name is taken over; a regular file or symlink there is never removed or followed, and that listener polls. With both names removed, or both now naming a second broker that bound the path afresh, shutdown dials nobody: it abandons the parked acceptor after a bounded run of unanswered rounds, counts it, leaves the second broker its names and not one stray connection, and still releases the log lock. A wakeup dial that connects counts only when this listener's own acceptor answers it: with the dial pointed at a stranger that takes every connection (as a 127.0.0.1:P socket beside a 0.0.0.0:P broker does on macOS, or a socket renamed over the path before a serve that bound the path itself read it), shutdown abandons after at most 10 dials (exactly 10 on Unix) instead of redialing forever, and leaves the stranger's socket path in place (unit tests in src/broker.rs). The negative control is the polling fallback (a socket path whose wake name exceeds SUN_LEN): its sleeps and wakeups are seen to count while idle. Broker and BrokerHandle stay Send + Sync (checked at compile time), as embedders sharing a handle across threads rely on. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features aead --test suite -- blocking_accept:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib wake_dial_another_listener` |
| `broker.last-value` | Last{filter, after, max} returns the newest record of each matching subject sorting strictly after `after` (at most max, in subject order), then Mark{next, head, resume}; the connection stays usable and Subscribe{from: next} continues with no gap or duplicate. Pages reassemble the one-page answer, which equals the last-per-subject fold of a from-0 replay and is byte-identical after a restart. max clamps to 4096 and each scan round visits at most 65536 entries; resume is empty or a subject the filter matches. Hidden /a/commit, /a/will and /a/bind never appear and are refused to client publish; a producer may create at most 4096 subjects; 16 subscribers each get 1000 records once, in order; a non-reading peer is torn down. Consistency is per page, not across pages; a subject superseded mid-walk is omitted, not stale; exhausting the 64 scan rounds is untested. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- last_value:: broker_core_regressions:: last_snapshot::` |
| `broker.last-walk` | Client::last_walk(filter, after, max, on_row) pages Last on the Mark's resume cursor until it comes back empty (never on a short page), handing each row to on_row; it returns the FIRST page's (next, head) and a continuation (None when the whole answer was delivered, Some(after) after Walk::Stop or `max`). Client::last_all collects the same walk, and asb's `last` uses it. Over a real broker with LAST_PAGE_MAX + 100 subjects every subject is returned exactly once in order; a write made between pages lands above the returned head and a Subscribe from its next delivers it; Stop, max paging, max=0 and on_row errors end the walk exactly and leave the connection usable. Against a scripted peer: empty pages with a live cursor are walked through, a page longer than it asked for is refused as InvalidData with none of its rows delivered, and LAST_WALK_PAGES_MAX (4096) requests without an end is an error, never a short answer. The page bound is exercised against a scripted peer, not a real broker; rows are a union of per-page snapshots, so a reader that tails folds newest-wins. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- last_walk::` |
| `broker.last-view-retire-history` | Checked owned retirement history for `Last` (`LastView`, `LastStep::Pending`, `LastViewError`, `LastViewGarbage`, `BrokerLog::last_view_at`, `BrokerLog::publish_last_visible`, `BrokerLog::last_matching`), backed by Clean-Origin (`I5`) `Astream.Broker.TlaLastView` (`last_view_available_advance`, `last_view_prune_clamp`, `last_view_not_before_avail`, `last_view_not_above_pub`, `last_view_window_admits_code`, `last_view_prune_within_quantum`). `publish_last_visible` is an owner visibility publication (`PublishedBackwards` below `published`, `AbovePromoted` above `head`), never a query-driven prune; `last_view_at` admits only `[available, published]` (`TooOld` below `available`, `NotPublished` above `published`), and `last_matching` rejects `WrongOpen`, `ChangedFilter`, and `CursorBackwards`. Future-observable forward `OnceLock` links let a live `LastView` retain pre-retire payloads retired after its pin while the store detaches its own visible prefix (constant work when fully visible, bounded `PRUNE_WORK = 64` steps when partial, iterative `Link::drop` chain destruction outside external log locks) and charges retirement preparation + subject-index visits to one shared `scan_max` step (`LastStep::Pending`). Long-lived views and returned `Arc<BrokerRecord>` handles retain their explicit memory cost until read or dropped. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib last_view:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib twins:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- last_snapshot::` |
| `broker.fetch-bounded` | Fetch{from, filter, max} is a bounded, non-terminal read: unlike the streaming verbs it answers in the request loop and hands the connection back. It delivers at most max matching records with offset >= from, scanning at most FETCH_SCAN_MAX = 65536, then Mark{next, head} with next after the last record scanned, so a page matching nothing still advances. Over real Unix sockets, max=3 pages a 10-record log once in order and the connection then publishes again; max=0 returns the head; on a 70 000-record log with a sparse filter the first page stops at next = 65536 (the cap, not max) and paging reaches every match once. Hidden /a/commit records are scanned, never delivered; pending pipelined acks are flushed first. A bound on work per request, not a rate limit; the Replicated tier's lagging head is exercised only via the store's `visible` argument. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- fetch::` |
| `broker.agent-native-fork-and-cognition` | Two agent-native delivery verbs built on the broker's subscribe primitive. ForkSubscribe streams an alternate timeline -- the topic forked at offset N with that one record swapped -- to a sandbox consumer while the live log and subscribers are untouched (a fresh live subscriber still sees the original record at N); no producer is re-run. A real astream-agent CogRecord (tool_use) published to /a/cog/<session>/tool_use/<class> reaches an auditor subscribed to /a/cog/*/tool_use/secret for every session's secret class and not the others, and the delivered bytes decode to the original CogRecord. Fork-delivery is a frozen snapshot whose stream ends, not a live counterfactual; routing is by publisher-assigned subject, not message content. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- agent_native::` |
| `broker.true-exactly-once-e2e` | A consumer group's committed offset lives on the broker (rebuilt from the log's commit annotations on open), so SubscribeGroup resumes past committed records and never re-delivers them, including across a full broker restart. process_and_produce appends the output and commits the input offset in one durable record (one fsync), deduped by (producer_id, producer_seq): a retried transaction, as after a crash before the ack, appends no second output and applies no second commit (a sentinel published after the retry is the next output delivered). The reserved /a/commit subject is broker-internal: its records consume offsets but are never delivered, even to /a/>, and a client publish, read-process-write output, or fork replacement there is refused with nothing appended and the idempotency key unconsumed. Single broker, single durable log; no cross-partition transactions. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- exactly_once_e2e::` |
| `broker.bench.publish-floor` | The broker's durable-publish hot path -- publish over a Unix socket, acked only after the Strict per-message fsync -- holds a throughput floor (BENCH_N=500), a regression gate on the write path. The comparison with Redis (AOF appendfsync=always) is a separate, machine-specific attached artifact (evidence/bench/broker-vs-redis.md, via scripts/bench-vs-redis.sh), not asserted by this command. | `BENCH_N=500 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_microbench` |
| `broker.group-commit-durability` | The broker routes every mutation (publish, commit, process_and_produce) through one writer thread that batches queued records under a single fsync and acks each only after that fsync, so an ack still implies fsync'd. Under concurrency (16 producers x 50), acked offsets are distinct over a gap-free spine, a re-sent (producer_id, producer_seq) is deduped to its original offset, and a shutdown plus reopen recovers every record without duplicates. Through the log's fault-injection seam, a failing batch fsync tells every op in the batch 'commit failed', leaves the head unmoved and subscribers unaware, and rolls the file back to the durable prefix; once the fault clears the same keys append anew, and a failed rollback truncate poisons the log until reopen. Shutdown stops the writer by queuing a stop behind its work, never by an idle poll: an idle writer sleeps on its queue with no timer, so across 24 acked publishes, the last listener's shutdown and an unserved broker's drop BrokerStats::writer_wakeups stays 0, both stops return under a one-minute hang detector, and the next open holds every acked record; and a stop queued while the writer is held inside a batch, between one op queued before it and one after, ends the writer only once both ops are committed and answered at their offsets (unit test writer_stop). Single durable log; open fsyncs the recovered file and its parent directory every time, which this command does not witness. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- group_commit:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib writer_stop` |
| `broker.poisoned-refusal` | A POISONED log says so, to the embedder and to every producer, in words astream owns, with no new code and no request tag: Broker::poisoned names the reason (a full flush that failed with acked or held records no full flush covers, or a rollback that could not put the file back), and None on a healthy or reopened log; while it is poisoned the writer answers EVERY op of a batch, whichever connection sent it and not only the first, with an ERR_REFUSED refusal that starts proto::LOG_POISONED_PREFIX and carries the reason (a waited will or binding keeps those words first), and refuses flush_durable the same way; BrokerRefusal::is_poisoned reads it and needs both the code and the prefix. Over a real socket: on Relaxed with an escalation table, a failed full flush answers its own op 'outcome unknown', which is not is_poisoned; two wills queued from two connections behind one parked batch (Broker::pause_next_batch, the queueing read off writer_round_trips) are both refused is_poisoned, naming the reason, and so are a resend, a fresh publish, a commit and flush_durable; the reopen names no poison and moves the generation. THE CURE an embedder relies on, that reopen: the op whose outcome was unknown, resent under its pinned (producer_id, producer_seq), lands EXACTLY ONCE, within one boundary named below. Over the socket, in the same boot (here on Relaxed, and in ordered_tier's a_failed_full_flush test on Ordered where it is served and on Relaxed), the reopened log holds the record the failed flush held back, read back from the page cache, and the resend dedups to it: offset 1, deduped, the head and visible head still 2, a Fetch holding it once with the body first sent; negative control: another body under the same key dedups to offset 1 too (dedup is by key, not body) and a fresh seq appends, deduped false. The branch no socket reaches, the device having DROPPED the record, is the crash simulation's (crash_sim::the_outcome_unknown_resend_after_a_poison_lands_exactly_once, on Ordered and Relaxed): the held batch carries the escalated publish and an ordinary one behind it, both outcome unknown and both resent in order; EVERY power-loss image of every device state from the one before the batch's write through the poison (every subset of each epoch prefix's blocks, with and without its size change: the test asserts the enumeration was whole, not a sample, and prints how many images it recovered) is recovered in the next boot, and so is the same-boot reopen; on each the generation moved under the same id, or, where no record survived, the log took a fresh id (the id itself differs); where a record was cut, the dedup map the open rebuilds holds no entry for its key and its resend appends it fresh at the cut point (the recovered head, in order), the escalated one owing and taking its full flush, and a flush is owed exactly then; where it was kept, its resend dedups to it and is acked at once; deduped is answered exactly when the recovered log holds the key, at that record's own offset, never for a record the recovered log lacks, and the log ends holding exactly one record per key, the one its resend was answered with; counted over the images of a state after the batch's write (one before it dropped nothing), each tier reaches both branches, the cut one with the record before the batch kept and with the escalated body still on the image (a torn batch the recovery cut), and Relaxed also keeps the escalated record and cuts the one behind it, each asserted. Planted breaks, each run and reverted: an open that forgets the dedup map fails the socket tests and the simulation at the same-boot reopen; an open that rebuilds dedup over records its recovery then cut (an escalated frame intact in a torn batch) fails the simulation on an Ordered power-loss image; an open that keeps an empty log's id fails it at the fresh-id check. THE BOUNDARY: the same-boot dedup is acked at once because the reopen's flush wrote the record, which holds in these tests (the injected fault fails the flush before it reaches the device, which therefore still holds the record for the reopen's full flush to make durable). A real device's failed flush can leave them clean but unwritten (fdatasync after EIO, PostgreSQL's fsyncgate; on Apple a drive-cache reset after the barrier sent them), and the reopen's flush then has nothing to rewrite, as BrokerLog::flush_failed_lossy states: the dedup acks a record that may be on no media, and a later power loss that takes it is REFUSED at the next open (damage below the floor) until asb repair cuts it, loud and never silent, but the acked op has then landed zero times. No test here reaches that case. A Strict rollback that fails poisons the log and says so the same way. Negative controls: a Strict batch whose flush fails is rolled back, code 5 and not is_poisoned, with Broker::poisoned None, and the same connection's next write lands; a code-8 refusal that starts with the prefix, and the wording an older broker used ('append failed: log poisoned (...)'), are not is_poisoned. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- poisoned_log:: ordered_tier::a_failed_full_flush_refuses_the_waiting_op_and_poisons_the_open && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib -- crash_sim::the_outcome_unknown_resend_after_a_poison_lands_exactly_once` |
| `broker.hello-health` | A broker says, in the answer to every Hello and with no request tag spent, whether its log is POISONED and what its flushes did: extension-block entry 0x04 (HelloHealth, Client::broker_health) carries a poisoned flag, the reason (cut to HELLO_REASON_MAX bytes at a character boundary) and the flush counts since the open (barrier, full, skipped, failed). The poison, the latch to Strict and the counts live in per-open vitals the log writes and the broker reads WITHOUT the log lock — the poison set once, the first reason standing — so Broker::poisoned, Broker::served_durability, Broker::flush_counts, Broker::wait_poisoned and the Hello answer never wait on the writer. The poison is PUBLISHED LAST: on a double fault (an Ordered batch on a device that refuses its barrier, whose full flush and rollback both fail), on the writer's batch and on the un-batched append alike, <log>.id already records Lossy at the instant the poison is published (a seam reads it there; a mutant that publishes at the rollback reads the identity the open recorded and fails), the rollback's reason stands, and the tier told beside the poison is the latched Strict. A latch a failed flush sets rides its poison: with the copy Hello answers from held stale across an escalated flush's failure (it still says Relaxed), the answer names the poison and served Strict, LatchedAfterFlushFailure (a mutant that tells the copy's tier fails); a barrier's latch is told on its own; a second poison changes neither the reason nor the tier (a mutant that keeps the last reason fails). And none of it waits on the writer: with the writer parked inside a batch HOLDING the log lock (read off the mutex with try_lock), a poisoned Strict log's poisoned(), flush_counts().failed == 1, wait_poisoned and a fresh connection's Hello naming the same reason all answer under a one-minute hang detector (a mutant poisoned() that takes the lock hangs past it). The codec: the entry round-trips healthy, poisoned and poisoned with an empty reason; a 1023-byte reason followed by a 3-byte character is cut before it; flag bits past bit 0 and bytes after the reason are ignored; a non-UTF-8 reason or a body cut inside its fixed part or reason is malformed; a frozen copy of the block reader from the release before 0x04 reads a block carrying it as the same tier and lineage, healthy or poisoned, and refuses one whose health entry is cut short (the negative control). Over a real socket, on Relaxed with /e/> escalated: healthy (no poison, failed 0, the counts Broker::flush_counts reports, served Relaxed), then InjectedFaults::sync fails an escalated publish's full flush, then a fresh Hello names Broker::poisoned's reason word for word, the tier served Strict with LatchedAfterFlushFailure, and failed = 1; the reopen reads healthy. Negative control: a Strict batch whose flush fails is rolled back, and Hello says failed = 1 with no poison. wait_poisoned, its waiter seen parked first (a seam counted under the vitals' lock), wakes with the reason at a failed rollback's poison and with None when the last listener shuts down, each under a one-minute hang detector against an hour (a shutdown that does not wake the vitals fails), and returns None at once on a healthy broker with a zero timeout. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib -- proto::tests::the_health_entry broker::tests::the_poison_and_the_flush_counts_are_read_without_the_log_lock broker::tests::a_hello_that_names_the_poison broker::tests::wait_poisoned store::ordered_tests::a_double_fault store::ordered_tests::the_latch_rides && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- broker_health::both_faces broker_health::a_rolled_back_write` |
| `broker.settle` | A broker names its log's DEVICE in the answer to every Hello and SETTLES it on request, so a writer outside the log whose own file is on that device orders its writes by a barrier and knows them on stable media by the broker's full flush instead of draining the device again with one of its own (DESIGN-aterm-fabric §2.1, aterm critic item F07). Extension-block entry 0x06 (LogDevice, Client::log_device) carries the log file's st_dev, read by fstat on the descriptor the log is written through, and a flag that its full flush drains the device: set on Apple, where the flush is F_FULLFSYNC, which fcntl(2) says persists data fsync'd on the same device before it, an F_BARRIERFSYNC included; clear elsewhere. LogDevice::settles trusts the drain only for that device number. Request::Settle (0x19, announced by bit 8 of the request set, REQUEST_SETTLE, Client::supports_settle) goes through the writer in send order and is answered in the ack stream (Client::send_settle pipelined, Client::settle blocking) with the durable head after a full flush the writer issues once it took the request, WHATEVER already covers the head (FlushAsked::Always), where Broker::flush_durable skips a covered head. On a guarded broker it needs a genuine unexpired read-write grant of any filter (CapPred::AnyWrite). The codec: the entry round-trips with its flag set and clear and at the extremes of st_dev; flag bits past bit 0 and bytes after the flags are a later build's, read as this entry; one cut inside its device or before its flags is malformed; a frozen reader of the release before the health entry reads a block carrying it as the same tier and lineage and refuses one whose length runs past the frame (the negative control); an unknown tag (0x7f) is still skipped; Settle's tag number is pinned and no undeclared request tag decodes. Over a real socket on Relaxed (no horizon, no escalation): Hello announces the settle and names the log file's st_dev, draining exactly on Apple; a settle behind a publish is one full flush answered with the durable head past it; a settle on a log a full flush already covers is one more full flush with none skipped; pipelined behind a publish on one connection the settle's answer covers that publish; the CONTROL, flush_durable on the same clean log, is skipped and counted so with no full flush. On a guarded broker an unattached connection and a read-only ring are refused where the ack would be (the refusal pays it) and a read-write grant of another subtree is answered with one full flush. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib -- proto::tests::the_device_entry proto::tests::the_tag_budget proto::tests::the_nonce_extension_block && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --all-features --test suite -- settle::` |
| `broker.health-sidecar` | A broker keeps its operator snapshot (REQUIREMENTS R5) beside its log in a format astream owns: <log>.health, a header line then key=value lines (BrokerHealth: pid, written_at_ms, log identity, tier asked and served and why, base, head, visible and durable heads, records, bytes, subjects, producers, pending wills, group commits, replica, connections, parked tails, the four flush counts, the writing process's flush tally as the write took it (process_flush_full, process_flush_barrier and process_flush_plain: astream_sys::flush_tally, every flush the process asked astream-sys for since it started, an embedder's as well as the log's), the log's residency (resident_records and resident_bytes: what of the retained history the residency tier keeps decoded, every record with no budget), the writing process's flush times (process_flush_clock, process_flush_full_ns, process_flush_barrier_ns, process_flush_plain_ns, process_slow_flushes, process_slow_flush_ring and process_flushes_running: astream_sys::flush_times, each kind's total nanoseconds, the clock they are on, how many flushes were slower than 1 ms, the last 64 of them as index:kind:start-end, oldest first, - for none, and how many calls were still running as the times were read; claim os.flush-times), poisoned and its reason), mode 0600, written whole to a fresh temp (never through a link planted at its name) and renamed over the sidecar, never flushed. It is written at the open (OpenOptions::health_every, Broker::keep_health), every period, at once when the log is poisoned (whenever the snapshot it last wrote did not name the poison), and a last time after the stop's clean close; Broker::write_health writes it on demand and is refused once the broker has stopped. The writer holds an advisory lock on each file it publishes until it replaces it or the log closes, so read_health tells Serving (the lock is held) from Gone (nobody holds it: the broker closed or died) without a pid guess, and Unknown where no advisory locks are kept. Proven over real files: the text round-trips every fact, the reason on one line, and a later build's extra key is skipped, and an older build's snapshot (the same header without the tally's three keys, or without the two residency keys) is read with that fact unknown (process_flushes or resident None, never zeros) and renders back to the same text, while another header (likewise without the seven flush-time keys: process_flush_times None), a missing key, a tally missing one of its three keys, a residency missing one of its two, a value that does not parse, flush times missing one of their seven keys (an E09c build's, without process_flushes_running, among them), a slow-flush ring entry that ends before its start, names no flush kind, has no end or names no index (an E09 build's ring), an index not past the one before it, an empty ring value, a ring holding an index at or past its count or more than 64 entries, a clock no build reads, a poison flag without its reason and a reason without the flag are refused; read_health names a file under another first line by that line, and one under this build's own header by the fact missing or not parsing, never quoting the header as another format; a published file reads Serving, a rewrite replaces it whole and still reads Serving, the released file reads Gone with its last facts; a broker's sidecar carries its retained records, subjects, pending will, group commit, connections, parked tail and heads, and a tally of each kind between this process's readings before and after the write, its Strict publishes' full flushes among them (a snapshot that carried no tally fails), its flush times: each kind's total between this process's readings before and after the write, on this build's clock, the slow count between them and every slow flush it names one this process's ring held (a snapshot that carried none, or zeros, fails), and its residency: every retained record with no budget, and after Broker::set_resident_budget(0) the newest record alone of the same retained four, as Broker::residency says (asb stats prints resident_records and resident_bytes; rendered from an older broker's snapshot, which names no residency and no tally, its served line prints - for both residency keys and the three tally keys, and from one naming an empty residency and a zero tally it prints those zeros, the negative control); on Relaxed with /e/> escalated and an hour-long period, the sidecar written at the open reads healthy and Serving under this pid, and after InjectedFaults::sync fails an escalated flush it names the poison an hour before its period (a mutant writer that does not wake at the poison fails), served Strict (LatchedAfterFlushFailure) and flush_failed = 1; the stop leaves it Gone with the poison as its last word; the reopen reads healthy. A poison that lands after keep_health's first (healthy) snapshot and before its thread's first wait is named under a one-minute hang detector against an hour's period (a mutant that looks at the poison before each wait, instead of at what it last wrote, sleeps the hour and fails). A Relaxed broker stopped with three page-cache records leaves a Gone last word with head 3 and durable_head 3, as the reopen finds, where its serving snapshot said durable_head 0 (a stop that writes its last word before the clean close fails). Negative control: a Strict rollback writes failed = 1 and no poison. `asb serve` keeps it (--health-ms, default 2000; 0, a word or the flag on another verb is exit 2, no log opened) and `asb stats` on a log a running broker holds — which it cannot open — prints the snapshot's counts, heads, connections, served tier, flush counts, the serve process's flush tally (process_flush_full counting its Strict publishes' full flushes), writer=serving, the serve process's pid and poisoned last; once the serve is SIGKILLed the log opens and stats prints the offline line. Negative controls: a killed serve's sidecar beside a log another broker now holds exits 1 naming the dead pid, stdout empty; no sidecar at all exits 1. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib -- health:: broker::tests::a_poison_after_the_snapshot && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- broker_health:: cli_health:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --bin asb -- an_older_brokers_snapshot_prints_its_residency_and_tally_unknown` |
| `broker.model-based-equivalence` | An in-memory reference model (a Vec plus dedup and group-commit maps; it shares the implementation's staging vocabulary, so it specifies the same interface rather than being a foreign oracle) runs in lock-step with the file-backed group-commit BrokerLog over many seeded random operation sequences, compared after every staged op, flush, simulated crash, and final reopen: head, every durable record, group_start per group, and both dedup maps. The mix exercises the commit-subject dedup exemption, offset assignment, group-commit monotonicity, and refusal of /a/commit publishes. A crash drops the log with an unsynced staged batch and cuts the file mid-frame or zero-fills the tail; the reopened log must match the model's crash rule. Std-only, seeded, reproducible. It claims no whole-system or agent-behaviour correctness. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib model_tests` |
| `broker.bench.groupcommit-floor` | The group-commit write path holds a concurrent durable-throughput floor (32 producers, each awaiting a Strict fsync-before-ack) that fails if batching breaks, since one fsync is amortized over a batch. The absolute floor catches a regression to fsync-per-message only on hosts where an fsync costs milliseconds; with sub-millisecond fsync or a tmpfs log directory the un-batched rate itself clears it, and the gate detects only a catastrophic slowdown. | `PRODUCERS=32 BENCH_N=200 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_groupcommit_bench` |
| `broker.pipelining` | One connection keeps many publishes in flight: the broker splits it into a read half that submits to the group-commit writer and an ack-writer thread that streams acks back in order, joined by a bounded queue (PIPELINE_DEPTH) that back-pressures the client so per-connection memory is capped; in-flight publishes share group-commit fsyncs under the same Strict ack-after-fsync guarantee. The read half holds the writes it finds already received whole, one behind another, and hands them to the writer as one run, so what a client writes together (Client::send_pipelined, a burst of publishes and commits in one write) lands in one batch behind one flush, however long the read half spends checking each request; before this, a publish and a commit written together on an idle guarded Ordered broker took two batches 989 times in 1,000. In-process: 500 publishes pipelined on one connection (window 128) ack in dense request order with no duplicate; re-pipelining the same (producer_id, seq) range dedups to the original offsets; subscribing on the same connection flushes pending acks, then streams the records in offset order; after a restart every acked record is durable and re-delivered in order; a publish and a commit sent as one burst are one writer batch and one flush (a barrier on Ordered, a full flush on Strict), 20 rounds on each tier, guarded and unguarded, while the same two written apart around a held batch are two (the negative control); a burst owes one ack per op in burst order, a refused op paying its own, and a burst with an op over the frame cap is refused whole, owing nothing. Ordering is per-connection; cross-connection order is the writer's single offset spine. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- pipeline::` |
| `broker.fresh-pipelining` | FreshPublish is explicitly offered in Hello and uses ordinary publish authorization, stored records and durability. A held producer key, including an identical body, a staged key or a predecessor key, is refused instead of deduplicated. A refusal known during writer staging fences later Fresh requests on that connection before they append; reader-side malformed, subject, authorization and rate-limit refusals enter the writer in order, and queued operations retain the fence after their connection owner drops. The A/B/C regressions preserve A then stop at a held B before C, including a relay staged before the fresh check and a lost collision reply. A new connection can retry with new keys; lost success replies may replay payloads according to the caller's digest-replay contract and are never inferred successful. Late commit, flush and quorum errors latch before the next writer batch but do not undo an already-staged suffix. The client requires the offer, limits outstanding replies to 512 and a fresh burst to 16 MiB including frame headers, checks the whole burst before writing, and retains FIFO per-request replies. Protocol and client tests cover bounds, partial writes and unchanged ordinary Publish bytes; Unix socket tests cover error ordering, authorization, lost replies and reconnect, while direct writer tests cover lineage, queued lifetime, full-flush failure and reopen. A real follower quorum shortfall leaves ordinary publication usable while fencing later Fresh requests on the failed connection. An Apple Ordered escalation failure retains the already-staged suffix as uncertain and preserves poisoned-log refusal for the next batch. These are runtime regressions, not a compiler proof or a throughput claim. See docs/DESIGN-fresh-publication.md for the consumer obligations. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --all-features --lib fresh` |
| `broker.bench.pipeline-floor` | Single-connection pipelining (window 256, each publish awaiting a Strict durable ack) holds a durable-throughput floor far above the one-in-flight rate, failing if it collapses to a fsync round-trip per message. The absolute floor catches that regression only on hosts where an fsync costs milliseconds; with sub-millisecond fsync or a tmpfs log directory the synchronous rate itself clears it, and the gate detects only a catastrophic slowdown. The command names its tier and clears the Ordered horizon (DURABILITY=strict HORIZON_MS=, an empty HORIZON_MS being unset), so neither knob reaches it from the environment the runner passes on, and the metric is named by the tier served (the Ordered pipeline is broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-floor). | `DURABILITY=strict HORIZON_MS= WINDOW=256 BENCH_N=5000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_pipeline_bench` |
| `broker.bench.replay-egress-floor` | The delivery/replay egress path holds a throughput floor: a subscriber replays a 10 000-record backlog of 256-byte bodies from offset 0, with catch-up reads handing out shared Arc records and delivery frames built from the borrowed record, without a deep copy. At BODY=256 the floor allows ~50 us per delivery, so it gates only a catastrophic (~10x) egress slowdown, not a reintroduced per-record copy, which would surface only at large bodies this command does not run. | `BENCH_N=10000 BODY=256 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_replay_bench` |
| `broker.bench.last-floor` | The Last retained-state query holds a throughput floor over 10 000 subjects with 200 000 records of history (20 per subject), queried as 4096-row pages under a wildcard filter, every measured query returning a full page (a short page exits 2). The absolute floor catches a per-query walk of the history (~50x the work of the index range scan). Two same-run ratios are gated by their own claims, `broker.bench.last-page-cost-ratio` and `broker.bench.last-ingest-ratio`. These are single-machine rates and ratios: they do not bound the lock hold within a page, report no latency percentile, and a correct implementation on much slower hardware could approach the floor. | `SUBJECTS=10000 HISTORY=200000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_last_bench` |
| `broker.bench.last-page-cost-ratio` | A Last query costs what the page it returns costs: a 64-row page must run at least 8x faster than a 4096-row page (`broker_last_page_cost_ratio`; the ideal is 64x). A query that walked the whole history or the whole subject index would cost the same at either size and drive this toward 1, so 8 gates that structural regression and not the fixed per-request cost. Two same-run samples, so like every bench floor it is re-measured up to BENCH_ATTEMPTS times keeping the best sample; a real regression depresses every one. A claim of its own, not an exit inside the bench, so the re-measurement applies. | `SUBJECTS=10000 HISTORY=200000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_last_bench` |
| `broker.bench.last-ingest-ratio` | A Last query does not park the writer: durable publish throughput on a second connection while queries run must stay at or above 0.25 of the same run's idle rate (`broker_last_ingest_ratio`; observed near 1, since a query holds the log lock only long enough to clone a page of `Arc`s). A query holding the log lock for its whole duration would collapse it. Two same-run samples, so it is re-measured up to BENCH_ATTEMPTS times keeping the best sample; a real regression depresses every one. A claim of its own, not an exit inside the bench, so the re-measurement applies. | `SUBJECTS=10000 HISTORY=200000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_last_bench` |
| `broker.bench.fanout-floor` | Live fan-out holds a delivery floor: 64 subscribers attached to one filter before 20 000 records are published must each receive every record at its exact dense offset (a gap or short count exits 2). The absolute floor catches only a catastrophic (~10x) egress collapse and is a whole-process rate, since broker, writer and all subscribers share the process. The same-run ingest ratio at four subscribers is gated by its own claim, `broker.bench.fanout-ingest-ratio`. The same ratio at all 64 subscribers is reported but not gated; observers are not free, and the broker's cost is not separated from the co-hosted subscribers'. | `SUBS=64 BENCH_N=20000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_fanout_bench` |
| `broker.bench.fanout-ingest-ratio` | Delivery is off the commit path: durable publish throughput with four live subscribers must stay at or above 0.5 of the same run's throughput with none (`broker_fanout_ingest_ratio`). Fan-out done on, or synchronized with, the commit path costs the producer once per observer and divides this toward 1/4, so 0.5 gates that structural regression from both sides. The ratio is two single samples taken back to back, so machine contention can depress it with no regression at all (it slows one leg and not the other); like every bench floor it is re-measured up to BENCH_ATTEMPTS times keeping the best sample, and a real regression depresses every sample. It is a claim of its own, not an exit inside the bench, because a hard exit would bypass that re-measurement. | `SUBS=64 BENCH_N=20000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_fanout_bench` |
| `broker.relaxed-tier` | A Relaxed ack means the batch is in the OS page cache (no fsync): it survives a process crash but not power loss; Strict (the default) acks only after fsync. At the Relaxed tier, acked records get a dense in-order offset spine and are delivered in order, a re-sent (producer_id, producer_seq) is deduped to its original offset, and a graceful restart recovers a clean prefix with dedup intact. A Relaxed broker in a child process acks 100 publishes and is SIGKILLed; on reopen every acked record is recovered in order with dedup intact, and a torn tail appended on top is truncated. Recovery truncates only an incomplete or all-zero trailing frame; a complete frame failing its CRC mid-log makes open refuse with InvalidData, leaving the file untouched, and only BrokerLog::open_repair truncates there. Power-loss durability for Relaxed is not claimed: a power loss can lose any acked batch written since the last open's fsync or clean close, and not only from the end. What is claimed is that the loss is announced, tested on the images it leaves, each reopened in another boot as a power loss implies (Boot::Known): a log that came back shorter opens with its generation moved under the same id and lineage at its head (LogIdentity::continues answers it), its next append reusing the first lost offset under the new identity; a log with a zeroed hole above its durable floor and intact records behind it keeps the longest intact prefix, cut at the hole, and moves the generation the same way; damage below the floor (bytes the open's fsync made durable) refuses with InvalidData, the file untouched, until the explicit repair (`asb repair`, BrokerLog::open_repair) cuts there and moves the generation. The SIGKILLed child's log reopens with its identity unchanged, read through the real kernel's boot id, the log declared on the boot volume (its /tmp path may sit on a device of its own, where every unclean weak stop rightly moves the generation): a process crash moves nothing. A log sealed at rest is repaired only with its key (BrokerLog::open_encrypted_repair, broker.log-encrypted-at-rest): the keyless repair refuses it rather than cut it to nothing. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- relaxed_tier::` |
| `broker.bench.relaxed-floor` | The Relaxed tier keeps a single producer at or above 100 ops/s: an ack follows a page-cache write, with no flush on its path (it survives a process crash, not power loss). The floor is low on purpose. A correct Relaxed run slows while another process flushes the same volume in full: on the dev Mac (2026-10-26, M5 Max, macOS 27.0.1, the log in /tmp on the boot volume) it read 18082-63771 ops/s with no bench beside it (once 1874, at a 1-minute load of 14), 1364-2648 beside one Strict bench, 265-453 beside two and 262-279 beside three, where a Relaxed path that flushed every batch in full (the negative control: flush_owed's Relaxed arm returning Flush::Full) read 164-238 with no bench beside it. No floor tells that flush from a busy volume with any margin, so three other claims gate a flush on the Relaxed path instead, at any load: broker.bench.relaxed-full-flush-ceiling and broker.bench.relaxed-barrier-ceiling count the flush calls the process makes into astream-sys, which is every flush the broker makes but a full one through std::fs::File directly, whether or not Broker::flush_counts counts it, and broker.bench.relaxed-strict-ratio sees a full flush however it is made. This floor catches only a slowdown with no flush in it, below 100 ops/s (10 ms an ack), and is not a measure of how far Relaxed beats Strict, which broker.bench.relaxed-strict-ratio gates. The metric is named by the tier the broker served, read after the run, and the tier comes from one match with its label (unit-tested in the example's bench_tier module, which also refuses an unknown DURABILITY with exit 2); this command runs and gates only the relaxed tier, and clears the Ordered horizon (HORIZON_MS=, an empty HORIZON_MS being unset) so one the runner passes on cannot refuse the run. | `DURABILITY=relaxed HORIZON_MS= BENCH_N=20000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_durability_bench` |
| `broker.bench.relaxed-full-flush-ceiling` | The same single-producer Relaxed run issues no full flush: broker_relaxed_full_flushes_per_sec, the full_sync and full_sync_data calls the process made into astream-sys during the run (astream_sys::flush_tally) per second of it, stays at 0, so none an ack. The broker makes every full flush there but one through std::fs::File directly, and the tally counts each call whether or not Broker::flush_counts counts it. A Relaxed ack follows the page-cache write alone, the tier keeps no horizon, and the bench asks for no escalation, flush_durable or sync_full, so nothing in the run owes one; every run on the dev Mac read 0.00 (2026-10-26, from no bench beside it to two Strict benches flushing beside it; in a correct run of every tier the tally reads what Broker::flush_counts counts). The count is of calls the code makes, which a busy host cannot raise: a full flush after every Relaxed batch reads the ack rate, never 0, however slow the volume makes the run (the negative control, flush_owed's Relaxed arm returning Flush::Full, read 164.30-238.50 a second with no bench beside it and 86.31-104.22 beside one Strict bench; issue_flush's Relaxed arm calling the batch's sync_data itself, which Broker::flush_counts does not count, read 113.66 a second at 114 ops/s, a 1-minute load of 23), so this ceiling fails it at any load, where broker.bench.relaxed-floor cannot. A full flush through std::fs::File directly is no call into astream-sys and reads 0 here (that control, issue_flush's Relaxed arm calling File::sync_data on the log itself, read 0.00 at 262 ops/s); broker.bench.relaxed-strict-ratio fails it instead. Label and refusal rules are broker.bench.relaxed-floor's, whose command this is. | `DURABILITY=relaxed HORIZON_MS= BENCH_N=20000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_durability_bench` |
| `broker.bench.relaxed-barrier-ceiling` | The same single-producer Relaxed run issues no ordering barrier: broker_relaxed_barriers_per_sec, the barrier_sync calls the process made into astream-sys during the run (astream_sys::flush_tally) per second of it (F_BARRIERFSYNC on Apple; elsewhere a barrier is an fdatasync, counted as a barrier all the same), stays at 0. The broker is forbid(unsafe_code), so barrier_sync is the only way it can issue a barrier, and the tally counts each call whether or not Broker::flush_counts counts it; every run on the dev Mac read 0.00 (2026-10-26, from no bench beside it to two Strict benches flushing beside it). It gates what broker.bench.relaxed-floor's floor of 100 ops/s cannot: a barrier after every Relaxed batch (the negative control, flush_owed's Relaxed arm returning Flush::Barrier) read 2103-4596 ops/s with no bench beside it and 67-135 beside one Strict bench, under the 5000 that floor held before 2026-10-26 but mostly above its 100, and it reads the ack rate however busy the volume is (2102.52-4595.91 and 66.73-135.09 barriers a second; issue_flush's Relaxed arm calling the batch's barrier itself, which Broker::flush_counts does not count, read 3589.43 a second at 3589 ops/s, a 1-minute load of 15), so this ceiling fails it at any load. broker.bench.relaxed-strict-ratio does not: a barrier costs far less than a full flush, and that control read a ratio of 24.34 with no bench beside it. Label and refusal rules are broker.bench.relaxed-floor's, whose command this is. | `DURABILITY=relaxed HORIZON_MS= BENCH_N=20000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_durability_bench` |
| `broker.bench.relaxed-strict-ratio` | A single producer's Relaxed acks stay at least twice as fast as its Strict acks in the same run: broker_relaxed_strict_ratio, the Relaxed leg's publishes a second over the Strict leg's, on two brokers in one process (logs in /tmp) whose legs take turns, 100 rounds of 10 Strict publishes and then 100 Relaxed ones, each leg's time summed, so whatever slows the volume slows both legs within the same fraction of a second. A Relaxed ack that waits for a full flush costs what a Strict ack costs however the flush is made, so the ratio falls to about 1 at any load, where no Relaxed floor can tell that flush from a busy volume: on the dev Mac (2026-10-26, M5 Max, macOS 27.0.1, the boot volume) issue_flush's Relaxed arm calling the batch's sync_data itself read 0.92-1.02 from no bench beside it to three Strict benches flushing beside it, and calling File::sync_data on the log directly, which no flush count sees, 1.00; a full flush through std after every write of every tier, so that Strict pays two, read 1.58-1.83, which this floor fails narrowly. A correct run read 88.78-251.78 with no bench beside it, 13.40-13.85 beside one Strict bench, 5.11-6.92 beside two (5.11 at a 1-minute load of 44, another session's build running), 3.05-3.89 beside three, 2.84-2.88 beside four, 2.98-3.25 beside six and 3.00 beside eight. A barrier on the Relaxed path costs far less than a full flush and is broker.bench.relaxed-barrier-ceiling's to catch (it read a ratio of 24.34 here with no bench beside it). macOS only, where a full flush is F_FULLFSYNC and costs milliseconds; where one costs about what a page-cache write does (tmpfs, a fast fdatasync) a correct run reads near 1 too. Either broker serving another tier than its leg names exits 2. The legs' own rates, broker_ratio_relaxed_ops_per_sec and broker_ratio_strict_ops_per_sec, are reported and not gated. | `BENCH_ROUNDS=100 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_relaxed_ratio_bench` (re-run on macos only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `broker.bench.ordered-floor` | The Ordered tier configured with a 100 ms horizon (HORIZON_MS=100; astream's own default, Durability::ordered(), is one second) keeps a single producer at or above 30 ops/s: each ack waits for one ordering barrier (F_BARRIERFSYNC) and a full flush comes only once the horizon has passed since the first ack none covers, so an ack that waited for the horizon's flush instead (about ten a second) fails it. The floor is low on purpose. A barrier waits behind a full flush in flight on the device, so while another process flushes the same volume in full (a Strict broker, astream's own test suite) a correct run falls to about the Strict rate: on the dev Mac (2026-10-26) 1518-1970 ops/s at a 1-minute load of 8-17, 193-219 beside one Strict bench and 124-127 beside two, where HORIZON_MS=0 (a full flush after every batch, the stand-in for a fall back to a full flush per ack) read 433-448, 164-205 and 101. No floor tells that fall back from a busy volume, so the count beside it, broker.bench.ordered-full-flush-ceiling, gates it, and the two are set so a run that flushes in full after every ack fails one of them however busy the volume is: at 30 ops/s or more it flushes at least 30 times a second, above that ceiling of 20, and below 30 it fails this floor. Not a measure of how far Ordered beats Strict, which aterm's same-moment, same-disk ledger measures. The metric is named by the tier the broker served, read after the run: where the open serves Ordered as Strict (off Apple, off the boot volume, a barrier refused at the open) or the log latches to Strict during the run, the command prints broker_strict_ops_per_sec and this claim fails on its missing metric. A broker reporting another horizon than the one asked for exits 2, and so does an unknown DURABILITY, a HORIZON_MS for another tier and one that is not a whole number (the example's bench_tier module, unit-tested). macOS only: elsewhere Ordered is served Strict by design. Not a latency claim: it reports no percentile and does not say how late a horizon's flush comes. | `DURABILITY=ordered HORIZON_MS=100 BENCH_N=5000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_durability_bench` (re-run on macos only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `broker.bench.ordered-full-flush-ceiling` | The same single-producer Ordered run issues full flushes only for its horizon: broker_ordered_full_flushes_per_sec, the full_sync and full_sync_data calls the process made into astream-sys during the run (astream_sys::flush_tally, which counts each full flush the broker makes there whether or not Broker::flush_counts counts it) per second of it, stays at or below 20. The writer flushes in full once the horizon has passed since the first ack no full flush covers, so its full flushes begin at least 100 ms apart and the first 100 ms into the run: at most ten a second, whatever a full flush costs on the device and however busy the host or the volume is, since a slower run is a longer one and only lowers the count (6.3-9.1 a second on the dev Mac, 2026-10-26, from a 1-minute load of 8 to two Strict benches flushing beside it). A full flush after every ack reads its ack rate (HORIZON_MS=0 read 101-432 a second there), so this ceiling fails it at any rate above 20 ops/s, and broker.bench.ordered-floor fails it below 30. Label and refusal rules are broker.bench.ordered-floor's, whose command this is; macOS only. | `DURABILITY=ordered HORIZON_MS=100 BENCH_N=5000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_durability_bench` (re-run on macos only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-floor` | Single-connection pipelining configured with DURABILITY=ordered, HORIZON_MS=100 and WINDOW=64 holds 1500 ops/s over a run of 50 000 publishes, long enough to span several horizons, so an ack that waited for the horizon's flush (64 a horizon at most, about 600 ops/s) fails it. Low on purpose, as broker.bench.ordered-floor's is: beside another process's full flushes a correct run fell to 6676-7687 ops/s with one Strict bench flushing on the volume and 3849-3904 with two (45523-140086 at a 1-minute load of 8-17), at or below what HORIZON_MS=0 (a full flush after every batch) read beside them, 7805-8013 and 5718, so no floor tells that fall back from a busy volume, and broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-full-flush-ceiling gates it. A batch holds at most the window's 64 publishes, so a run that flushes in full after every batch at 1500 ops/s or more flushes at least 23 times a second, above that ceiling of 20, and below 1500 it fails this floor. The metric is named by the tier served, read after the run, so an Ordered request served or latched Strict fails on the missing broker_ordered_pipeline_ops_per_sec; the knobs are parsed by broker.bench.ordered-floor's bench_tier module, with the same refusals. No PRODUCERS=32 group-commit variant: aterm runs one pipelined connection per bridge. macOS only. | `DURABILITY=ordered HORIZON_MS=100 WINDOW=64 BENCH_N=50000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_pipeline_bench` (re-run on macos only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-full-flush-ceiling` | The same Ordered pipeline run issues full flushes only for its horizon: broker_ordered_pipeline_full_flushes_per_sec, the full flush calls the process made into astream-sys during the run per second of it, counted as broker.bench.ordered-full-flush-ceiling counts them, stays at or below 20, by the writer's horizon rule broker.bench.ordered-full-flush-ceiling states (at most ten a second at 100 ms, whatever a full flush costs and however busy the host or the volume is; 5.5-9.0 a second on the dev Mac, 2026-10-26, from a 1-minute load of 8 to two Strict benches flushing beside it). A full flush after every batch reads the batch rate, at least the ack rate over the window's 64 (HORIZON_MS=0 read 90-191 a second there), so this ceiling fails it at any rate above 1280 ops/s, and broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-floor fails it below 1500. Label and refusal rules are broker.bench.ordered-pipeline-floor's, whose command this is; macOS only. | `DURABILITY=ordered HORIZON_MS=100 WINDOW=64 BENCH_N=50000 targo --unverified run --quiet --release --locked -p astream-broker --example broker_pipeline_bench` (re-run on macos only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `broker.replicated-tier` | A leader opened with Broker::open_replicated (or over authenticated, encrypted cross-host links via Broker::open_replicated_transport / Broker::open_replicated_guarded_transport with FollowerTransport::{Plain, Sealed, Handshake, Identity}) writes to the page cache (no fsync) and ships every committed record, commit records and annotations included, to follower brokers at its exact offset; a follower refuses a gap or conflicting record, so its log is an identical prefix. Across untrusted LAN/WAN networks, FollowerTransport wraps every follower dial and reconnect in XChaCha20-Poly1305 (`Sealed`), forward-secret X25519+PSK (`Handshake`), or mutual Ed25519 signed-DH (`Identity`) with per-follower pinned host keys, rejecting wrong PSKs, MITM/unpinned follower identities, and live `revoke_identity` revocations. Under the `cap` feature, Broker::open_replica_guarded enforces capabilities on a follower's accept path and Broker::open_replicated_guarded* attaches an unbound read-write capability (Hello -> Nonce -> Attach -> Mark) on every follower link at initial dial and on reconnect after a follower restart, fencing links that refuse Attach or Replicate with ERR_UNAUTHORIZED and refusing bound or read-only follower capabilities up front with InvalidInput. An ack is given only once a quorum of followers holds the record in memory, so it survives loss of the leader node but not full-cluster power loss; only a Will registration is acked on local commit. Operator-invoked BrokerLog::truncate_replica_to / Broker::truncate_replica_to truncates a diverged replica back to a common prefix `[base..target_head)` (writing phase-1 `Cutting` under a freshly minted replica LogIdentity before resizing, rebuilding in-memory indices, and clearing divergence), and Broker::rejoin_followers un-fences fenced links on the leader's writer thread, redials, catches up `[base..head)`, re-adopts the leader's LogIdentity, and restores the quorum watermark in one batch.
