One static leader with static followers; no election or automatic failover. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test replicated && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- broker_core_regressions:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --test suite -- replicated_retained_open:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cap_hardening::replicated_guarded_attaches_follower_capability_and_recovers_across_follower_restart && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --all-features --test suite -- replicated_transport::` |
| `broker.sharded-routing` | A ShardedBroker runs N independent single-partition brokers (each with its own durable log, writer, group commit and pipelining; no cross-shard lock), routing by assign_partition over the subject so each subject's records land in one shard, in order. Tested in-process: re-sends are deduped per shard; distinct subjects spread across shards, each with a dense offset spine; a fan-in subscription merges every shard's matches exactly once with per-shard order; every shard recovers after restart. The shard count is persisted, and a reopen, connect, or fan-in with a different count is refused; a per-shard failure reaches the fan-in consumer as an Err naming the shard; dropping a fan-in subscription closes every shard socket and joins every forwarder. Ordering is per-partition, not global, and sharding is not a single-disk Strict throughput win. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test sharded` |
| `broker.tcp-transport` | The broker serves the same exactly-once pub/sub and resume protocol over TCP: connection handling is generic over the byte stream, and a TCP client drives publish, ordered exactly-once subscribe, resume from an offset across a disconnect, and dedup. Transport-independently, a connection that never completes a first frame is closed after the first-frame timeout while a quiet established producer is not, and simultaneous connections are capped: one beyond the cap gets an error frame and is closed, and a closed connection frees its slot. Plain serve_tcp is plaintext and unauthenticated, for trusted networks only; sealing and capabilities are separate claims (broker.sealed-tcp-roundtrip, broker.cap-enforced-on-attach). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- tcp_transport::` |
| `broker.cli.pub-sub-roundtrip` | The `asb` CLI (serve/pub/sub/commit/stats) round-trips bytes byte-exactly (through NUL and newline) over a real Unix socket, with `sub` framing pinned as `<offset> <nbytes>`, the raw body, then one newline. An explicit --id/--seq retry prints `<offset> dup`; a --seq without a stable producer id (--id or one bound grant) is a usage error. Flags are strict: non-numeric values, unknown or wrong-verb flags, a key or capability on argv, a surplus positional, a non-UTF-8 argument, or --from with --group exit 2 with nothing on stdout, and `--` ends flag parsing. A second `asb serve` on a live socket exits 1 while the first keeps serving. `sub --group G` resumes at the committed offset + 1, `commit` is monotone, and both survive a restart. `asb repair` truncates a log that `serve` refused for a corrupt record, reports the discarded bytes, and is idempotent; `asb stats <log>` reports log offsets, resident bytes, distinct subjects, consumer groups, bindings, and pending wills. With the cap feature, a --cap-file grant attaches: in-scope publish and subscribe succeed, a widened filter is refused. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cli_roundtrip::` |
| `broker.cli.tcp-roundtrip` | `asb --tcp` round-trips bytes byte-exactly over plaintext TCP (no confidentiality or peer authentication; trusted networks only), with framing pinned as in broker.cli.pub-sub-roundtrip; serve binds host:0 and prints the chosen address. With the aead feature, serve and both clients take the 32-byte PSK from an environment variable (--key-env) or a file (--key-file), never argv, and round-trip byte-exactly through the sealed wire; a --key-env naming an unset variable exits 2 rather than downgrading to plaintext, and a malformed key file is a usage error, not a panic. Both ends run on one host over loopback: this proves the CLI shape, not a two-machine run. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features aead --test suite -- cli_tcp::` |
| `broker.cli.fleet-verbs` | `asb` subprocesses (mint, --cap-file, last, fetch, drain, ack, pub --seq-file) run against a broker over a Unix socket, output pinned byte-exactly. last/fetch/drain print `<offset> <nbytes> <subject>`, the body, a newline; last/fetch end with `MARK next=<n> head=<h>`. fetch pages gaplessly from next; drain commits a group batch; ack appends answer and group cursor atomically, (a retry prints `dup`) under reserved sequences >= 2^63 that `--seq` may not use. mint refuses the unbound read-write cap in both spellings without --legacy-unbound; secrets and capabilities never come from argv; a bound grant supplies the producer id. `pub --seq-file` persists a write-ahead sequence and refuses an empty or non-numeric file. A crash can burn a sequence number or, since drain commits before printing, lose a batch; concurrent --seq-file use is not exercised; one host only. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cli_fleet::` |
| `broker.cli.fleet-sealed` | The fleet-verbs flow over an XChaCha20-Poly1305-sealed TCP wire (--key-file) into a capability-guarded broker, with two nodes holding rings minted by `asb mint`: A publishes presence under its bound grant; B reads it read-only; B posts to A's inbox with its own principal as `<src>`, while a forged `<src>` is unauthorized and never lands; A drains exactly B's message and acks once (the retry prints `dup`); B fetches `re=<offset> verdict=handled`. A wrong pre-shared key never attaches (exit 1, state unchanged), and an attach proof lifted from one sealed connection is refused on another (fresh per-connection nonce). A two-connection `drain` works with --key-env although the variable is consumed on first read. One host over loopback, not two machines; the guarded broker here is opened in-process (the CLI's `serve --secret-file` face is broker.cli.serve-guarded) and the replay case uses the library client. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features "aead cap" --test suite -- cli_fleet_sealed::` |
| `broker.cli.serve-guarded` | `asb serve --secret-file PATH \| --secret-env NAME` opens a guarded broker (asb built with `cap`), and repeatable `asb serve [log] --listen <scheme>:<addr>...` (REQUIREMENTS R2: `unix:`, `sealed:`, `handshake:`, `identity:`, and `tcp:` gated by `--allow-plaintext`) binds multiple listeners onto one shared log, capability guard, producer-sequence dedup table, and will table. Over real subprocesses and sockets: a capability `asb mint` sealed under the broker's secret publishes and fetches back; one minted under another secret, or none at all, exits 1 and lands nothing, while an unguarded broker accepts the capability-less publish; a record published on a Unix listener is fetched over sealed TCP and SIGMA/Ed25519 identity TCP at the same offset and dedups retries across transports; a will registered over sealed TCP fires into the Unix listener's `last`; `--listen tcp:` exits 2 without `--allow-plaintext`. Owner-only secret/key/host-key file checks, loopback enforcement without `--allow-remote`, and created-log cleanup on refused binds hold across both single-endpoint and `--listen` grammars. One host over loopback. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features "aead cap identity" --test suite -- cli_serve_guarded::` |
| `broker.cli.defect-regressions` | On the real `asb` binary over a Unix socket: 48 `pub --seq-file` runs on one file, six concurrent at a time, all publish as `new` with an exact counter; `last` follows the broker's resume cursor past its 4096-row page bound (4097 subjects), with --max still bounding and --after resuming; a cap-file grant containing a space attaches verbatim while its truncation is refused; `drain --idle 0` exits 2 before connecting; a dead stdout exits 1, not a panic, and write verbs restate the durable fact with `Do NOT retry`; a dead stderr keeps the exit status; `commit`/`ack` operands at or past the visible head exit 2, leaving the group intact, also on a guarded broker; an ack the library sent is deduped when `asb ack` retries it under the same producer id. Not covered: filesystems without advisory locks, or the 65536-entry scan bound; the fixture is published through the library. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test cli_status_and_range && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cli_defects::` |
| `digest.sha256-vectors-and-recorded` | astream-digest is the workspace's one SHA-256: FIPS 180-4 transcribed, zero dependencies, forbid(unsafe). It matches the FIPS 180-4 vectors (empty, abc, the 448- and 896-bit messages, one million 'a') and encodes a length above 2^32 bytes in the 64-bit length field. Every message length 0..=1024, fed whole and under random update splits including zero-length ones, answers the digests recorded from the sha2 crate it replaced (one SHA-256 over all 1025, plus the padding and block boundaries). aterm-digest's own sweep moved in with its HMAC (tests/lengths_and_streaming.rs): every length 0..=200 and ten large inputs up to 100,000 bytes answer one SHA-256 over them recorded against sha2, and random update splits up to 200,000 bytes answer the one-shot digest. The live comparison against sha2 ran when the crate landed and is not re-run: these are recorded answers. Not a constant-time or throughput claim. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-digest` |
| `digest.hmac-one-construction` | astream-digest holds the workspace's one HMAC-SHA256 (RFC 2104), HKDF-SHA256 (RFC 5869) and constant-time slice compare: the copies in aterm-digest (streaming HMAC and ct_eq), astream-cap (a const core) and astream-aead (a wiping HMAC) were four transcriptions nothing held to each other (aterm-net's fleet HKDF was the fourth), and astream-cap, astream-aead and aterm now call this one: aterm-digest re-exports HmacSha256, hmac_sha256_parts, hkdf_extract, hkdf_expand_32 and ct_eq with its own copy deleted (aterm cb93e33cff), and aterm-net's fleet keys derive through them (aterm a719a8ff7d). Three doors go through the same steps (key block and pads, primed inner hash, message, outer hash): the streaming HmacSha256 (new_from_slice, update, finalize, verify_slice), the const hmac_sha256_parts over a list of parts, and the non-const hmac_sha256_wiping, which zeroes its key block and both pads before it returns. All three answer RFC 4231 cases 1-7 (case 5 over its published 16 bytes) and astream-aead's answers for 64- and 65-byte keys, and a key with one bit flipped is another tag in every case; a 64-byte key is used verbatim and a 65-byte one hashed first. Where a message is cut into parts is not part of the tag (empty parts and the empty list included), and random splits of every length 0..=200 reach the one-shot tag through all three doors; aterm-digest's message- and key-length sweeps answer one SHA-256 over them recorded when they matched the hmac crate. verify_slice accepts the tag and refuses its 16-byte prefix, a 33-byte extension and every one of its 256 one-bit flips (for one key and message; the length sweep flips one random bit at each length); HmacSha256's Debug is exactly `HmacSha256 { .. }`, plain and pretty, for three keys. ct_eq is slice equality, refusing a one-bit difference at every position of a 32-byte tag and any length mismatch, all-zero slices 65,536 bytes apart among them in both orders (what a compare that folded only the length delta's low 16 bits answered equal); that its fold has no data-dependent exit is a property of its source, not measured. hkdf_extract and hkdf_expand_32 answer RFC 5869 A.1, A.2 and A.3 (the PRK and the first 32 OKM bytes; A.2's 80-byte salt is hashed first, A.3's salt and info are empty), an empty salt is 32 zero bytes, and the session key astream-aead recorded over the sha2 crate is reproduced from its raw inputs. The wipe is checked on the pads the wiping door's body (tag_then_wipe) is handed: after it returns the door's tag they are all zero, so a body without the wipe fails. That the stores survive codegen, or that no copy outlives the call (the SHA-256 states the pads were fed into are not wiped), is not claimed, and nothing here is a timing measurement. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-digest --lib -- hmac:: ct_eq && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-digest --test lengths_and_streaming` |
| `cap.unforgeable-mint` | A capability is a signed astream_wire Filter: the subject subtree its bearer may attach to. astream-cap mints it with HMAC-SHA256 (astream-digest's one HMAC, RFC 2104 over the workspace's own SHA-256, whose RFC 4231 vectors are digest.hmac-one-construction's; its tags, attach proof and producer ids are checked here against answers recorded over the sha2 crate it replaced, unchanged since the MAC moved) and verifies it with a tag compare that folds all 32 bytes with no data-dependent early exit -- asserted structurally on the private verify_diff that verify calls (for every ordered pair of tampered tag bytes the returned fold carries both differences), and moving the compare out of verify_diff fails the build via deny(dead_code). This is not a timing measurement; a wrong-length tag is refused on length alone. A genuine capability grants exactly its subtree; a widened filter with the old tag, a guessed tag, and the wrong secret are rejected. This covers who may attach where, not confidentiality (see aead.seal-open.authenticated). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap` |
| `cap.grant-mode-and-producer` | A capability is a signed grant string '[rw\|ro][,p=<principal>]:<filter>' with the HMAC over the whole string, so mode and bound principal are as unforgeable as the subtree. A bare filter parses as read-write unbound; 'ro:' reads its subtree but is refused publish and commit; 'rw,p=<principal>:' publishes only under producer_id_of(principal) (pinned on vectors). A flipped mode, swapped or dropped principal, or widened filter under a genuine tag is rejected, and Grant::parse fails closed on malformed input. attach_proof/verify_attach round-trip and refuse another nonce, grant, tag, secret, or proof length; the proof compare is asserted to fold all 32 bytes, structurally, not by timing. That the grant is parsed only after its proof matches is observed only on Linux, by memory: cap.attach-proof-before-parse. Library only (no broker; see broker.cap-keyring-enforced); producer_id_of says nothing about who issued a principal, and per-class principal name shapes are not enforced. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap` |
| `cap.attach-proof-before-parse` | On Linux, verify_attach checks the attach proof before it parses the grant, so the grant of a peer without a genuine proof is hashed, never parsed: a 16 MiB grant of 8M `/a` segments under a wrong proof is refused while the process's peak resident set (VmHWM in /proc/self/status) grows by less than 128 MiB; an unauthenticated grant of that size cost about 590 MiB before the check was moved ahead of the parse. Observed by memory, in a test binary of its own so no other test moves the peak; the bound is a coarse ceiling, not a count of parse calls. Linux-gated: astream's only gate is `make ci` on macOS, which reports this claim skipped, never verified, and nothing runs it on Linux. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap --test attach_parses_nothing_unauthenticated` (re-run on linux only; skipped elsewhere) |
| `cap.expiry-enforced` | A capability may carry a signed `exp=<unix-ms>` field in its grant prefix (each field at most once); malformed, repeated or unknown fields are refused, and older grant shapes still parse and never expire. Expiry is half-open (valid at exp-1, expired at exp) and an unparseable grant counts as expired. All four grant predicates reach authority through one private function that checks it: they refuse at and after the deadline while the capability still verifies, and editing the deadline breaks verification and the attach proof. `attenuate`, `attenuate_offline` (offline bearer-attenuated Macaroon chains via `via=<hex-parent-grant>` up to depth 8 without the mint secret), and `can_attenuate` derive strictly narrower child capabilities (`ro <= rw`, principal fixed or newly bound, filter contained, expiry shortened or added), and the broker verifies every link of a Macaroon chain on attach and authorization. Against a broker reading a real wall clock, an expired capability is refused at attach as `expired`, a future one works normally, and a connection attached while live loses authority once the deadline passes: checked per request, an open subscription (streaming or parked) ends with an `expired` error at the deadline of the grants that authorized it, and a registered Will is checked separately through the private disconnect handler with a controlled clock. Those tests register through real authorization and inspect the durable writer after its flush barrier: a Will fires before its capability deadline and is suppressed at and after it; a nonexpiring capability stays live at the maximum time. Certificate expiry remains independent, with a fresh clock read after the capability and peer-revocation checks, and the tests preserve the original short-circuit order, revoked-peer and principal suppression, and last-registration replacement. A client tells an expiry from every other unauthorized refusal by type: at the attach, on a request whose covering grants have lapsed, and at a stream's deadline the refusal is code 8 with a message starting `proto::CAP_EXPIRED_PREFIX`, and `BrokerRefusal::is_expired()` reads true; a capability never minted under the broker's secret (even one carrying a lapsed `exp=`), an unattached connection and a request outside a live grant are code 8 and read false. Over the `identity` transport, `n-<16 hex>` principals are bound to `identity_principal(peer_key)`, and live revocation (`Broker::revoke_identity`, `Broker::revoke_principal`, `Broker::load_revocation_file`, and `asb serve --revocation-file`, including automatic fail-closed `SIGHUP` revocation-file reload on Apple targets) refuses new handshakes/attaches, terminates open connections/subscriptions across all transports, and suppresses pending wills. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features "cap identity" --test suite -- cap_expiry:: cap_hardening:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features "cap identity" --lib -- broker::disconnected_will_tests::` |
| `cap.capfile-one-line-format` | `astream_cap::capfile` is the one capability-file format: a line is `<grant> <tag-hex>` as `asb mint` prints it; lines are trimmed, blank and `#` lines skipped, split at the LAST ASCII whitespace (a grant may hold a space), and the tag is exactly 64 hex digits. format_line and parse_line round-trip grants holding spaces and `#` over tags covering every byte value; wrong tag lengths and non-hex bytes are refused at the exact byte without echoing the tag; a malformed file reports its first bad line as `path:line`. The same source is compiled by path into a default (non-`cap`) asb and into aspump, under the same cfg, so its tests live beside it in astream-cap (capfile_tests.rs) and run once. The grant half is not validated here (Grant::parse and verify decide authority), and asb's end-to-end behaviour through it is pinned by the broker.cli.* claims, not this command. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap --lib capfile` |
| `cap.secret-file-owner-only` | `astream_cap::capfile` holds the one rule for reading a secret file (a mint secret, a pre-shared key, a host seed; a cap file only for a face that reads it through read_private_ring, since read_file, the plain read asb's and aspump's --cap-file use, reads a ring at 0644 too, while read_private_file and read_private_ring refuse that file): check_private(path), read_private_file(path), read_secret_file(path), read_secret_in(dir, name), read_secret_at(path), read_secret_at_narrowed(path) and read_private_ring(path), which asb and aspump compile in with the rest of capfile (both depend on astream-sys directly), and which asb's --secret-file (read_secret_file) and serve's --key-file (read_private_file) go through. Each opens the file once, read-only and non-blocking (astream_sys::open_nonblocking), and judges that open descriptor by astream-sys's one owner rule (owner_private: a regular file, owned by this process's effective uid, carrying no macOS access-list entry that allows anyone anything, with no group or other mode bit), never a stat of the path, and the readers return the bytes of that same descriptor: a check by path followed by a read by path resolves the path twice, so the file that passed need not be the file whose bytes are used. Over real files: modes 0600, 0400 and 0700 pass, both path readers then returning the file's bytes, and 0640, 0604, 0644 and 0660 are refused by each of the three path functions as PermissionDenied with a message that starts `<path> is mode <4 digits>: ` and names `chmod 600 <path>`; an absent file is NotFound from each; read_private_file returns the file's bytes exactly with no length floor (17 bytes and an empty file come back as stored); read_secret_file returns the file's bytes exactly, a trailing newline and a trailing space included (the newline-terminated secret is not its trimmed text), and refuses 31 bytes as InvalidData naming the byte count, and 17 bytes and an empty file as InvalidData, with SECRET_MIN = 32. On macOS a 0600 secret carrying an `everyone allow read` entry, open to every user though its mode says otherwise, is refused by all four functions as PermissionDenied starting `<path> carries an access list (ACL) that lets other users in` and naming `chmod -N <path>`; after `chmod -N` the same file reads, and a deny entry passes. A FIFO at the path (mkfifo -m 600) is refused by read_secret_file and read_secret_in as InvalidInput, exactly `<path> is a FIFO: a secret file is a regular file`, without waiting for a writer, each behind a one-minute hang detector (with O_NONBLOCK removed the same test fails at the detector: run by hand when this landed, not by this command). An owner-only directory at the path is refused by the three path functions as InvalidInput, exactly `<path> is a directory: a secret file is a regular file`, before a byte is read. read_secret_in is the rule for a secret kept in a directory of its own: the directory is opened without following a link and held to owner_private_dir (a directory of ours, no ACL allow entry, 0700 or tighter), the name is one path component, and the file is opened O_NOFOLLOW and must have exactly one link. Over real files: a 32-byte secret in a 0700 directory reads exactly; a symlink to it is refused (PermissionDenied, `<path> is a symlink: `) and so are both names of a second hard link (`<path> has 2 links: `), while read_secret_file follows the symlink and reads the hard link (the path API is unchanged); a 0755 directory is refused as PermissionDenied starting `<path>: its directory <dir> is mode 0755: ` and naming `chmod 700 <dir>`, without calling the secret exposed (a 0600 secret with one link in it was readable by nobody else; the file's own refusals say `treat its contents as exposed`); a symlinked directory is refused; a name of more than one component (`sub/x`, `..`, `.`, empty) is InvalidInput; a missing directory or file is NotFound, a missing file even in a 0755 directory (the directory is judged when there is a secret in it to keep, so a caller that makes a missing secret after fixing its directory is told it is missing); `/` (holding `/usr`) is refused for its owner (uid 0) unless the reader is root, then for its mode; and on macOS a 0700 directory carrying `everyone allow list` is refused naming `chmod -N <dir>`, and reads once it is cleared. read_secret_at(path) is read_secret_in over path's own directory (`.` for a bare name) and file name, naming the path exactly as given: the same secret reads, a symlink to it and its 0755 directory are refused the same way, a missing bare name is NotFound starting `<name>: ` (never `./<name>`), and `/`, an empty path and `x/..` are InvalidInput. read_secret_at_narrowed(path) reads as read_secret_at will once the directory is 0700, for the dry run of a caller that narrows the directory before it reads: a 0600 secret in a 0755 directory reads (read_secret_at refuses the same read), while a 0644 secret there (naming `chmod 600`), a second link, a symlinked directory and, on macOS, an `everyone allow list` entry on the 0755 directory (naming `chmod -N <dir>`) are still refused, and a missing secret is NotFound: only a directory refused for its mode bits alone passes, which astream-sys's judging the mode last makes exact (os.cordon). read_private_ring(path) is read_file for a face that holds its rings private: one open, non-blocking and refusing a link at the path, judged by owner_private, then parse_text over that descriptor's bytes. It holds the file and only the file: over real files in a 0755 directory, modes 0600 and 0400 read what read_file reads, and a second hard link, an empty ring and a comment-only ring read too (no directory, link-count or length clause), while 0640, 0604, 0644 and 0660 are refused as PermissionDenied starting `<path> is mode <4 digits>: ` and naming `chmod 600 <path>`, a symlink read_file follows is PermissionDenied exactly `<path> is a symlink: a private ring is read through no link, so name the file it points at`, a FIFO is refused as InvalidInput exactly `<path> is a FIFO: a secret file is a regular file` behind a one-minute hang detector, a directory as InvalidInput, an absent file is NotFound, and bytes that are not UTF-8 or a malformed line are InvalidData, the line named `<path>:<line>: `; on macOS an `everyone allow read` entry is refused naming `chmod -N <path>`, and the same file reads once it is cleared. With the door's open and judging replaced by a plain read those four tests fail, the FIFO's at its detector (run by hand when this landed, not by this command). refusal_of(e) names the clause a reader refused a ring for, Refusal::Link or Refusal::File(NotPrivate) with its mode, kind or Acl, and is None for an absent file, a malformed line and an open the OS refused (mode 000, as this user: PermissionDenied too, its message starting `<path>: `), so a caller can tell a file it cannot open from one the rule will not take. Every refusal starts with the path: an OS error, which names no path (the raw error of the same open is checked not to), is re-wrapped `<path>: <error>` with its kind kept, the path appearing once, for the open of a missing file; a read error past the open is wrapped the same way by the code, and no real file is made to fail a read here. The owner, mode and ACL clauses are cfg(unix) (the ACL clause macOS only: a Linux POSIX ACL that grants anyone shows in the group mode bits) and are exercised on this Mac only; off Unix the kind and length are what is checked. That the rule and the bytes come from one descriptor is the code's structure, not a race this command runs: nothing here races a writer; read_secret_in judges its directory by path and then opens the file by path beneath it, so the directory's own parent decides who could swap it between the two. asb's composition (`--secret-file <path>: ...` and serve's `--key-file <path> ...`, the path once) is broker.cli.serve-guarded's. aterm's fabric reads its mint secret through read_secret_at (aterm-link's read_mint_secret: `aterm link broker --secret-file`, `aterm link mint`, `aterm fabric on` and `mint-for`), and `aterm fabric on --dry-run` through read_secret_at_narrowed when its root step would narrow the root, reads every cap file through read_private_ring (aterm-link's bridge::read_cap_file; a bridge its aterm supervises, which must not stop for a ring it has run on, asks refusal_of at its start and reads a ring refused for anything but its kind past the rule, through open_nonblocking, saying so), and aterm-uds judges each private file by astream-sys's owner_private and each private directory by owner_private_dir_by_path (one lstatx_np of its path on macOS arm64, no open), opening the directory and judging the descriptor by owner_private_dir where the path's attributes cannot decide, and an open refused there by kind_and_owner_of, so aterm's own copies of these rules are gone. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-cap --lib capfile_tests::secret_file` |
| `broker.cap-enforced-on-attach` | A guarded broker (Broker::open_guarded) requires every connection to attach a capability that grants each subject it publishes and contains each filter it subscribes (Subscribe, SubscribeGroup, ForkSubscribe alike); out-of-grant requests, un-attached connections (for every verb), and forged or widened capabilities (refused at the attach) are all refused, while the scoped subtree is delivered; a connection that has not attached within the first-frame timeout is refused and closed, however much it talks. Consumer groups are named as subjects the capability must grant: Commit, the commit half of read-process-write, and SubscribeGroup are each refused when either the group or the filter/output is out of scope, and work when both are in scope. A Last page's resume cursor is absent or a subject the requested filter matches, never an out-of-grant index entry. Enforcement is off by default: without the `cap` feature no secret can be set, and default-broker claims are unaffected. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cap_enforcement:: broker_core_regressions:: guarded_preauth::` |
| `broker.prepared-open-ownership` | Prepared plain and guarded broker opens run sidecar preparation after acquiring the actual log-file lock, before recovery and identity writes, retaining that file through the returned broker, or, when the open finishes an offline seal's switch a crash interrupted, the sealed log it moved over the name, which it locked before the rename, so the name stays locked from before preparation through the returned broker (offline_seal::tests::a_finished_switch_hands_back_the_sealed_log_locked, run by broker.offline-seal's command). Busy opens do not call preparation. A preparation error preserves its error kind and cause before recovery mutates a torn tail, and error or unwind releases the lock. Empty guarded secrets refuse before file creation or preparation at both durability tiers. These controls require a filesystem that supports the existing advisory lock; preparation side effects are not rolled back. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- prepared_open::` |
| `broker.guarded-durability` | A guarded broker takes the durability dial: Broker::open_guarded_with(log, secret, Durability), and Broker::open_opts with guard = Some(secret) and no escalation table, each open at Strict, at Ordered { horizon: None } and at Relaxed. Each reports the tier asked for and the tier served (Ordered served Strict off Apple, reason NotApple) and no rules, refuses an un-attached publish and a sibling's, and acks the grant's own publish at offset 0: served Strict, durable at its ack for exactly one full flush; served Ordered or Relaxed, neither. open_guarded stays the Strict shorthand. The guard does not read the tier: on a Relaxed guarded broker an un-attached publish, an out-of-grant publish or subscribe, and a capability minted under another secret (refused at the attach) are all refused while the grant's own subtree publishes and is delivered; an empty secret is refused at Strict, Ordered and Relaxed, by open_guarded_with and open_opts alike, before the log is created. Through the log's fault-injection seam, with every fsync of the log failing, a Relaxed guarded broker acks a pipelined batch and a lone publish, moves its head, delivers them, and a reopen recovers exactly them; open_guarded and open_guarded_with(Strict) answer the same publish 'commit failed' with the head unmoved, and land it once the fault clears. Power-loss durability for the Relaxed tier is not claimed; a power loss is announced instead, by the generation the first open after the reboot moves (broker.unclean-generation). aterm opens its fabric broker through open_opts with a guard (aterm-link durability::open). Since ordered/5, aterm-link's Durability::options hands every weak tier its escalation table (durability::escalation) and a named strict none, Durability::tier opens a named ordered as astream Ordered at aterm's horizon (durability::ORDERED_HORIZON, 100 ms) and a named relaxed as Relaxed, and durability::default_for makes Ordered the default of a bare guarded broker, the one aterm fabric on runs, on every platform, leaving to astream where it is served: astream serves it Ordered on Apple with the log on the boot volume and a barrier the device accepts at the open, and Strict elsewhere, with the reason. An aterm before ordered/5 (0.107.0 and older) handed it no table: 0.106.0 and 0.107.0 served their ordered as Strict, and older ones open Strict only. That is the open the every-tier run makes: a person's halt or mail on such an aterm's relaxed broker was acked on the page-cache write like any other record. A guarded broker with aterm's table is tested here and nowhere else in this tree: open_opts with a guard and that table (Subject /f/*/fleet/>, Subject /f/*/in/p/>, PrincipalPrefix h-, Group /f/*/cur/p/>), at Ordered { horizon: None } and at Relaxed as aterm opens it, and at Strict, with the table's size in Hello. The table is read from the fixture tests/fixtures/aterm-broker.escalate (parse_escalation_file), which aterm-link's astream_fixture_tests holds equal to durability::escalation, and its horizon line to ORDERED_HORIZON: the table tested is the one aterm opens, and a change to either side fails aterm-link until the other moves with it at aterm's next pin of astream. broker.ordered-tier's brokers are unguarded and bind nobody, so their principal rule never matches. Over a real socket with real capabilities: an un-attached publish is refused. A bound rw,p=h-andrew grant's first attach binds through the writer (one writer round trip, no bind settled, one hidden /a/bind record visible at the attach's ack) and the bind is not itself escalated (its producer is not yet bound when the record is staged). The bound producer's publish in a later batch is escalated: Broker::durable_head is above its offset at the ack, and FlushCounts.full rose by exactly 1. The same bound producer's will registration in the batch after that, one hidden /a/will record visible at its ack, is not escalated: a hidden record escapes the principal rule even when its producer is bound to h-. A bound rw,p=n- grant's publish on its own lane is not escalated (the durable head at or below it, no full flush), and its mail onto /f/F/in/p/... is. The unbound god cap's publish on /f/F/fleet/x is escalated, and since it has no principal only the subject rule can do that. A group commit on /f/F/cur/p/x is escalated. At Strict every one of those records, the bind and the will included, is durable at its ack for exactly one full flush. Negative controls, a fresh broker per dropped rule at Ordered and at Relaxed: without PrincipalPrefix h- the person's publish is not durable at its ack, and without either subject rule or the group rule exactly that rule's record is not, every other record unchanged. Off Apple, Ordered is served Strict (reason NotApple), and those runs assert that serving, with every record durable at its ack. That branch runs only off Apple; the Strict run executes its expectations on every host, since the write path reads the tier served. Durability is read after the ack arrives, so these corroborate broker.ordered-tier's race-free held-head check rather than replace it. aterm's 100 ms horizon is not exercised: it only adds full flushes on a timer and never reads the table. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- guarded_durability::` |
| `broker.guarded-at-rest` | aterm's broker, sealed at rest, without the sealed wire. On aterm's own configuration (guarded_at_rest; aterm's escalation table and Ordered horizon read from tests/fixtures/aterm-broker.escalate and its retention rules from aterm-broker.retain, the fixtures aterm-link's astream_fixture_tests holds equal to aterm's): opened with an at-rest key and require_bound_at_rest, the guarded broker with aterm's table escalates exactly what the plaintext one does, record kind for record kind, at Strict, Ordered (no horizon) and Relaxed (guarded_durability's drive_on, the same expectations). At Ordered { 100 ms }, Relaxed and Strict, keyless and keyed, guarded, with aterm's table on the weak tiers, its rules scaled and its health sidecar kept: a person's mail is durable at its ack; a retention tick on a hand clock cuts below the mail and a presence row, which the held store and the carried state keep (a fetch from 0 reads the mail, Last answers the row); the health sidecar names the log's identity, Serving while it serves and Gone after a clean stop; a reopen reads the mail, the row and the newest record back under the same head and identity. Negative controls: the sentinel scan of the log, <log>.state and <log>.held finds each file's sentinel in the clear on the keyless arm and no sentinel on the keyed one, and <log>.health holds none on either; bound, a deleted <log>.seal refuses the open (InvalidData, naming .seal), which opens again once it is back; a keyless open of the sealed log refuses it. A sealed log damaged below its floor is refused and repaired with its key at Ordered { 100 ms } as at Relaxed and Strict (at_rest::a_sealed_log_is_repaired_with_its_key_and_keeps_its_authentic_prefix). Not claimed: anti-rollback, which is Strict-only and refused at any other tier before the file is created, and is not involved here. MEASURED 2026-10-27 on an Apple M5 Max (18 cores), release, one --features at-rest build, the machine quiet by `aterm ctl @self await sys quiet for=20s` and the 1-minute load beside each number. broker_pipeline_bench at Ordered { 100 ms }, window 64, one connection, 200,000 publishes of 64 bytes, AT_REST unset against AT_REST=1, A B B A twice: off 143,128, on 68,878, on 68,282, off 61,101 ops/s (load 5.75, 5.75, 5.69, 5.55), then off 63,674, on 69,468, on 48,763, off 68,132 (load 5.55, 5.51, 5.51, 5.39); medians off 65,903 and on 68,580 ops/s. The barrier-bound pipeline (some 1,560 ordering barriers per 50,000 publishes) spreads by more than the seal costs, so at this tier no cost is resolved. At Relaxed, where nothing waits on the device: off 982,369, on 478,059, on 484,401, off 911,215, then off 946,155, on 442,816, on 399,584, off 258,275 ops/s (load 7.45 throughout, 7.25 at the last): the seal about halves a CPU-bound pipeline. The open of a 256 MiB retained log (broker_open_bench: 64,237 records of 4 KiB over 64 subjects, the sealed copy 258.5 MiB, both in the page cache, opened at Ordered { 100 ms } A B B A, run twice): plaintext 185.1 and 161.8 ms, sealed 326.8 and 309.6 ms (load 7.25); plaintext 185.8 and 162.7 ms, sealed 327.6 and 308.7 ms (load 6.99): sealed 1.83 times plaintext, about 145 ms more per 256 MiB, paid at every open because each authenticates the whole retained log, the in-place update reopen (ExeWatch) and the exit-75 reopen of aterm included. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap,at-rest --test suite -- guarded_at_rest:: guarded_durability:: at_rest::` |
| `broker.at-rest-without-sealed-wire` | aterm's broker, sealed at rest, without the sealed wire. astream-broker's at-rest feature names its cipher, dep:astream-aead, and none of the features that compile the sealed TCP transport (aead, handshake, identity), and anti-rollback still implies at-rest: at_rest::at_rest_names_its_cipher_never_the_sealed_wire reads the crate manifest, and built with --no-default-features --features at-rest neither Broker::serve_tcp_sealed nor Client::connect_tcp_sealed exists (at_rest::no_sealed_wire: each path resolves to a stand-in trait's item only because the type has none of its own, so the module would not compile were either built), nor astream-aead's SealedStream, the record layer and hello handshake the sealed wire speaks: astream-aead compiles it only under its own stream feature (default for astream-aead alone; the workspace dependency turns astream-aead's default features off, and astream-broker's aead names astream-aead/stream), and at_rest::no_sealed_wire::no_sealed_stream glob-imports astream_aead beside a stand-in SealedStream, a name that would be ambiguous were astream-aead to export one (planted, at-rest = [dep:astream-aead, astream-aead/stream] fails it, E0659). `targo tree -p astream-broker --no-default-features --features at-rest -e features -i astream-aead` names astream-broker's at-rest feature and no feature of astream-aead, neither default nor stream; with --features aead it names astream-aead's stream feature under astream-broker's aead. The manifests before showed astream-broker's aead feature under at-rest (at-rest = [aead]), and then astream-aead's default feature under at-rest = [dep:astream-aead], when SealedStream compiled with no feature asking for it. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --no-default-features --features at-rest --test suite -- at_rest::` |
| `broker.sealed-crash-prefix-law` | The crash simulation's power-loss law (broker.crash-sim.prefix-law's default sweep: the 2-batch exhaustive set at 16-, 32- and 64-byte blocks, then the seeded random sample, a sample crashed twice) holds on a keyed log, every open taking the key and the older build's prefix sealed too: 3,032,530 recoveries in the exhaustive sweep and 292,622 in the random one, none refused, every property held, with acked records lost (Ordered among them) and every loss moving the identity; its rot arm holds keyed at every tier. The simulated device keeps no <log>.seal, so those records seal under the v1 index-only AAD; v2's differs in the AAD alone, which changes no frame's size or place.  | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features at-rest --lib -- crash_sim::the_power_loss_law_holds_on_a_log_sealed_at_rest crash_sim::rot_in_a_log_sealed_at_rest` |
| `broker.offline-seal` | A plaintext log is sealed at rest offline, never mixed and never lost: BrokerLog::seal_plaintext(path, key) (at-rest, unix) opens the plaintext log under its lock (a broker serving it refuses the seal, AlreadyExists, nothing staged) and closes it cleanly, stages a sealed copy at <log>.sealing (every retained frame's payload sealed at its own offset under a fresh v2 <log>.seal binding, so the base, the offsets and the batch marks stay byte for byte; .state and .held re-sealed frame for frame as they stand, retention's compaction untouched; .base as it is; the identity sidecar closed at the same base and head, fingerprinting the sealed newest frame), and READS IT BACK before anything of the plaintext log is touched: opened keyed and bound, its records, broker times, base, head, identity, lineage, carried state and held store compared with the plaintext log's, and every frame of it, its .state and its .held opened under the key and compared byte for byte with the plaintext each was sealed from. Then a recover-forward marker <log>.sealmove is made durable, pinning the plaintext log it was written for (its length, its newest frame's fingerprint, and a fingerprint of each of its .base, .state and .held, or that it has none), and the staged files are renamed over the log's, the log last. Every open of a real log, in every build, settles first under the lock, after the embedder's prepare: a durable marker finishes the switch while the log is still the one it pins (the open locks the staged log before renaming it over the name and keeps it as the log, so no other open can lock the name in between: a_finished_switch_hands_back_the_sealed_log_locked), a marker whose log changed after it refuses the open (InvalidData, naming the marker and what changed) and moves nothing, a staging no marker covers is undone, and a marker that does not read refuses the open with nothing touched. Tested on a log as aterm's broker leaves one (rules set, a binding, a pending will, a group commit, a quiet subject and an unread mail below a rules cut, records after it): the sealed log reads back as the plaintext one (every record at its offset, the identity, every Last row, the binding, the will, the group's resume point, the held mail), a resend of the newest key dedups, a keyless open and another key refuse it, a second seal refuses (sealed already); an empty log seals. A KEYLESS OPEN OF A LOG A .seal SITS BESIDE REFUSES BEFORE IT READS ITS RETENTION SIDECARS (a_keyless_open_of_a_sealed_log_refuses_before_it_reads_held): a sealed log with .held and no .state, its first held frame resealed until its nonce reads, keyless, as rules cut above the base (about one sealed .held in 256 does), refuses a keyless open (InvalidData, naming .seal and the keyed open), .held byte for byte as it was, and a keyed open then reads it whole. A LOG WHOSE .seal IS GONE, WHICH THAT CHECK CANNOT SEE, KEEPS ITS .held THROUGH EVERY KEYLESS WAY IN (a_sealed_log_whose_seal_is_gone_keeps_its_held_through_every_keyless_way_in): the store's read finds the same cut and owes it until nothing refuses the open, so a keyless open, a keyless repair and a seal each refuse on the log's scan (InvalidData), .held byte for byte, each seen cutting it to nothing before the cut waited; with .seal back the key reads the log whole. A keyless open that CREATES the log clears a .seal a deleted log left, as it clears that log's other sidecars, so the plaintext log it creates opens keyless again and seals; beside an anti-rollback watermark the .seal stays, for a verified open to read (a_keyless_create_clears_the_seal_a_deleted_log_left). A retry of a seal a crash stopped at any step from its marker on refuses InvalidInput, sealed already, and the log reads back sealed whole (a_retried_seal_past_its_marker_says_sealed_already). A SWITCH IS FINISHED ONLY OVER THE LOG ITS MARKER PINS (a_switch_is_never_finished_over_a_log_that_changed_after_its_marker): a seal stopped after its marker, or after .seal and .base moved, then the log opened by a build that does not settle (this build with the marker and the staging moved out of its sight, as an older astream never looks) that acks a record, or adds a rules frame to .held with the log file unchanged: the next open, keyed and keyless, refuses (InvalidData, naming the marker and the log's length or .held) and the directory stays byte for byte as it was, the acked record in it; when that build only read the log, the next open finishes the switch and the log reads back sealed whole. THE CRASH LAW: a crash after each of the 12 durable steps (staged, sidecars, read back, marker, each of five sidecar renames, the log's, the directory flush, done), as a process crash and as every directory power_loss.rs says a power loss can leave (33 power-loss images, 33 holding a switch half done, 6 a staging with no marker), opens wholly plaintext before the marker and wholly sealed from it, whether the first open is keyless or keyed and bound: no staging is left, `.seal` exists exactly when sealed, every acked record, the identity and the state read back, and a log left plaintext seals again. Negative controls: the sentinel scan finds each of the log's, .state's and .held's sentinels before the seal and none after; a staged copy altered in one record, one held frame or the carried state, each sealed validly, is refused by the read-back (does not read back), the plaintext log untouched and readable; the refusals (no log: none created; a log a broker holds; a replica; a log beside a .hw watermark), the replica and .hw refusals leaving the log closed cleanly where it was left open before. Planted breaks, scratch-reverted, each failing: an open whose settle does nothing, a switch made with no marker, a read-back that only opens the copy. Seen failing against the code before each fix: the stale switch finished by a keyed open, the acked record dropped; a retry past the marker refused InvalidData; the name lockable between the switch and the open's relock; the replica and .hw refusals leaving the log open; the keyless open cutting that .held to nothing; a keyless open refusing the plaintext log a keyless create made beside a deleted log's .seal. Honest boundary: the switch unlinks the plaintext bytes, it does not erase them, so a snapshot, a backup or the device's freed blocks can still hold them; the .health snapshot (counts and an identity) stays as the last broker wrote it. MEASURED 2026-10-27 (broker_open_bench, release, Apple M5 Max, the machine quiet by `aterm ctl @self await sys quiet for=20s`): seal_plaintext of a 256 MiB plaintext log (64,237 records of 4 KiB) took 1,404.8 and 1,408.0 ms (load 7.25 and 6.99), measured before the marker pinned the plaintext log (the pin adds a walk of the frame headers and a hash of the newest frame and the sidecars, and the switch reads them back once; not re-measured). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features at-rest --lib -- offline_seal::` |
| `broker.check-open` | A check of whether an open would serve a log, which writes nothing: BrokerLog::check_open(path, key) takes the open's steps in the open's order and none of its writes. It opens the log read-write, as the open does, but non-blocking, writing nothing through it and never creating it (no log is NotFound, and the folder is left empty; a folder is refused IsADirectory, the open's own kind), takes the log's lock as an open does (a log another open holds is refused AlreadyExists) and releases it on return; judges an offline seal's switch and a compaction a crash interrupted as the open's settle and recovery judge them, without settling them (a marker that does not read, a log changed since its marker, a log that starts with neither frame a compaction's intent names, or a compaction whose finish would refuse, its base sidecar at neither base or its carried state gone, refuses as the open refuses; a switch the open would finish, or a compaction whose rename landed, is answered Unsettled, naming the step, only once every refusal of that settle is ruled out, so the open's settle can then fail only by a write, and judged no further); under a key checks the binding as the bound open does (a .seal that does not authenticate, the wrong key, or a keyed log with no .seal refuses, InvalidData), and keyless refuses a log beside a .seal; reads and authenticates the base, the carried state and the held store (opened read-write, as the open opens it for its later appends) and the identity sidecar, so a log or a held store at mode 0400, which the open cannot open for writing, is refused PermissionDenied as the open refuses it, the folder as it was, and back at its own mode is answered Opens (the control); and scans and classifies the log exactly as the open's recovery does, refusing a corrupt record with the open's own error text and counting, not cutting, a torn tail. On logs with every sidecar an aterm broker leaves (.id, .base, .state, .held, and sealed .seal), plaintext and sealed, every file in the log's folder is byte for byte what it was after every answer, Opens, Unsettled or a refusal, with no file added or removed, and Opens carries the base and head the open then serves. NEGATIVE CONTROLS on the same logs: a Strict open closed at once, the probe a caller made before the check, rewrites <log>.id; the open cuts the zero-filled tail the check counts (torn = 64), and finishes the seal's switch (crashed after its marker) and the compaction (crashed after its rename) the check names, removing <log>.sealmove and <log>.compact; and a switch over a log appended to after its marker and a compaction crashed after its rename whose base sidecar then reads neither base are refused with the open's own error text, not named. Not covered: the anti-rollback watermark (<log>.hw) is not read, an embedder's prepare verdict is not taken, and a write the open makes that the folder refuses (it replaces <log>.id through a temporary and a rename) or that the volume refuses is not foreseen. In a test or debug build, BrokerLog::seal_plaintext_stopped_at_its_switch makes the log a crash just after an offline seal's marker leaves, which the check answers Unsettled(SealSwitch) and an open finishes, for an embedder's tests. seal_path, beside identity_path and health_path, names the <log>.seal sidecar for an embedder. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features at-rest --lib -- check_open::` |
| `broker.cap-keyring-enforced` | Attach is a proof of possession: Hello returns a fresh 32-byte per-connection nonce and Attach carries HMAC(tag, nonce + grant); a missing Hello, a forged proof, and a proof replayed on another connection are refused. The ring holds up to 16 grants and authorizes existentially across them. Every such refusal comes back typed ERR_UNAUTHORIZED (permanent) and still reads `unauthorized`. Client::attach_all presents a whole ring: eight grants each authorize their own request; a forged grant mid-ring returns that refusal with the connection in step (the grant ahead of it attached and its read is answered as its own); an empty ring sends nothing; and, in-process against a scripted stream, every Attach goes out under ONE Hello, each proof over its nonce, before any acknowledgement is read. A read-only grant cannot publish or commit; a bound read-write grant publishes only under producer_id_of(its principal), an unbound one under any id. Replicate needs an unbound link grant and cannot inject hidden /a/will or /a/bind records into a log the broker owns. Bound attaches record a hidden id -> principal table, rebuilt on open, that refuses collisions; it is only as strong as who may write /a/bind. The Will verb's binding is not asserted here. The nonce is not claimed cryptographically random, the tag still sits in the capability file, and a compromised broker can forge anything. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- cap_keyring:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --lib client::tests::attach_all` |
| `broker.attach-settled-bind` | A guarded attach asks the writer to bind a principal only the first time: a read-write grant whose principal is already durably bound to its producer id, below the visible head, is answered from a copy of the binding table the broker keeps beside the log, with no writer round trip, no empty batch and no log lock, and Hello's log identity comes from a copy too. Counted, not timed, through BrokerStats (writer_round_trips, writer_batches, empty_batches, binds_settled) and Broker::writer_round_trips, which reads without the log lock. With aterm's node ring, the eight rules of GrantCeiling::aterm_node_ring that aterm mints (four rw,p= grants of one principal; the test builds its ring from them and checks that count, so it is the ring an aterm bridge presents and not a copy), a new principal's first connection makes 1 round trip in 1 batch, 0 empty, 3 grants settled, and each later connection 0 round trips, 0 batches, 4 settled, still publishing as that principal. Broker::pause_next_batch parks the writer inside a batch with the log lock held: a bound principal's Hello + whole attach and Broker::log_identity complete in that window; a first-time principal's attach queues its Bind behind the parked batch (an attach seen to finish before then fails at once), and once the batch resumes it completes with its last ack naming head 3, so it was answered only after its own /a/bind record (offset 2) was visible, with one round trip counted. Negative control: four different new principals make 4 round trips. Eight racing first attaches of one principal append one /a/bind record and answer every rw grant once, with no more round trips than connections. A batch's new bindings are copied before any of its acks is sent, which is what holds a connection to one round trip: Broker::pause_next_acks parks the writer with the batch committed, its head published, the log lock released and no ack sent, and in that window a second connection of the just-bound principal attaches with 0 round trips and 4 grants settled. A different principal on a bound id still goes to the writer and is refused as a collision; the principal stays settled across a restart and past retain_before (which refuses to drop the binding) and retain_before_carrying_state (which carries it), before and after another restart. That a replica's adopted identity reaches the copy Hello reads is broker.log-identity's tests. The copy is trusted while the log is open, because a promoted binding is not undone then: records are append-only, the first binding of an id wins, the committed tail is never cut back, and retention keeps every binding. Across opens nothing is claimed beyond what the open recovered, from which each open seeds its own copy. On a poisoned log a binding copied before the poisoning still attaches with 0 round trips and its publish is refused 'log poisoned', while a first-time principal's attach is refused by the writer ('the producer binding was not persisted') after 1 round trip and 1 empty batch. A batch pause armed on a broker whose writer stopped without reaching it answers wait with false. No guarded Replicated broker exists, so the below-the-visible-head rule is unexercised there. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap,retention --test suite -- attach_settled_bind::` |
| `broker.targeted-tail-wake` | The broker's writer wakes the parked subscriber tails (one notify_all on the log's Condvar by default, or per-subscriber `notify_one` when `OpenOptions::selective_tail_wake` / `Broker::set_selective_tail_wake(true)` is enabled) only after a batch that made a record some subscriber could be delivered visible: in the Strict and Relaxed tiers the batch's own new records, in the Replicated tier what the quorum watermark newly passed, whichever batch appended it. A batch that made nothing visible, or only hidden records (a group commit, a will registration, a retirement, a guarded attach's first bind), wakes no tail; in the Strict and Relaxed tiers that is every batch that appended nothing (a deduplicated retry). Counted, not timed, through BrokerStats (tail_broadcasts, selective_tail_wakes, tail_wakeups, parked_tails, writer_batches, empty_batches), with the liveness poll set to an hour (Broker::set_liveness_poll) so no poll timeout lands in a count: with four parked tails a commit, a will, a retirement and two deduplicated retries make 0 broadcasts and leave all four parked, while each deliverable publish makes 1 broadcast and resumes each tail exactly once; a lone delivery's publish-then-commit (aterm's bridge commits its inbox group once per burst, so a delivery nothing is queued behind pays a commit of its own) makes 1 broadcast and 4 wakeups per message over 20 messages (41 batches); a Replicated leader and its follower each broadcast for a publish and not for the commit after it; a Replicated leader whose publish is refused for want of a quorum (its one follower down) does not broadcast for it, and once the follower is back the later batch whose ship confirms it — a deduplicated retry, which appends nothing, or a group commit, which appends only a hidden record — broadcasts once, and the leader's tail, parked under an hour's poll, delivers the record; a first bind wakes nobody. A tail parked past hidden-only batches still ends at shutdown and at its capability's lapse, and its idle marks still name a cursor past two commits with nothing deliverable published. With selective tail wakeups enabled (`cost study B4 step 2`, Clean-origin `TlaTailWake`: `tail_wake_head_advance`, `tail_skip_clamp`, `tail_wake_is_caught_up`), 1 matching subscriber (`/m/>`) + 16 disjoint subscribers (`/n0/>`..`/n15/>`) across 200 publish+commit messages emit 0 broadcasts, 200 `selective_tail_wakes`, and 200 total `tail_wakeups` (1.0/msg instead of 3,400), a subsequent `/n11/hi` publish skips `[0..400)` in O(1) with 1 wakeup, wildcard filters (`*`, `>`) and live runtime toggling (`false -> true -> false`) lose zero wakeups, and `safe_skip_to` advances both cursor and `Told` across compacted non-matching prefixes without emitting a false `Event::Gap`. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap,retention --test suite -- tail_wakes::` |
| `broker.peer-probe-nonblocking` | A subscriber tail probes its peer for having gone at most once per liveness poll (200 ms, Broker::set_liveness_poll), whether it spends the poll parked or reading records it does not match, and the probe does not block: the socket is switched to non-blocking for the probe's one read and back, since std has no per-call MSG_DONTWAIT and the crate forbids unsafe. Counted through BrokerStats peer_probes: three tails that match none of twenty deliverable records read every one of them and make 0 probes under an hour's poll, where the previous rule made 60; a parked tail whose 5 ms poll comes due does probe, and a subscriber that left is reaped (its connection and its parked tail gone) both while parked and under a stream of records it does not match. Under the stream only the probe after a batch can reap it: idle marks are an hour apart (an idle mark's write would fail on the closed socket), the poll is 1 s, and each record is sent once the tail has read the last one and parked again, so a park lasts one turn of that loop and runs out its poll only if the host stalls the loop for a second; with that probe removed the test fails at its hang detector. The probe leaves the stream blocking: after several probes, a subscriber that reads nothing until 64 records of 60 KiB are committed then reads all 64 intact; in-process, a probe on a socket with no read timeout returns at once, the same end then writes 1 MiB before its peer reads a byte, and a closed peer reads as gone. With the restore removed both of those fail, and with the switch removed the in-process probe blocks until its hang detector. A vanished subscriber is noticed within about a poll, never sooner, and no latency is asserted here (examples/broker_wake_bench.rs measures it). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- peer_probe:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib broker::tests::the_peer_probe_neither_blocks_nor_leaves_the_stream_non_blocking` |
| `broker.unlocked-batch-write` | The broker's writer writes and flushes each batch with the log lock RELEASED, and that moves nothing a reader or a producer can see. A commit is three halves: a locked begin (offset check, seal, anti-rollback pre-bind, the flush the tier owes, the staged bytes handed out with the writer's own descriptor), the write and that flush with the lock free, and a locked end that promotes the batch only if the flush returned Ok, or rolls it back. Broker::pause_next_write parks the writer at the start of a batch's write: there a Fetch page, a Last page, stats and a new subscription's catch-up all answer, each seeing the log as it was before the batch (three records, head 3), and once resumed the batch's ack names offset 3, the same Fetch reads four records and the subscription is delivered the fourth, in one writer batch. Negative control: a try_lock of the log mutex fails at Broker::pause_next_batch (staged, the lock held) and succeeds at pause_next_write. The fault-injection seam travels with the batch from its begin: armed before it and cleared in its write window, the batch's flush still fails, nothing is promoted and the resend lands at the same offset; armed only in the window, it fails the next batch, not this one; and the rollback of a failed flush meets the seam the batch began with too, so a truncate fault armed in the window does not poison the log, and poisons it on the next batch. While the batch is in flight the log refuses every other mutation (staging, an un-batched append, a second begin, the clean close, retention: 'a batch is being written outside the log lock'), and Broker::retain_before waits on the writer's batch lock instead (BrokerStats::retention_waits 0 -> 1), compacting after the batch: offsets 1..=3 dropped, the batch's own record among them, where a run in the window could have dropped only two. A writer that died holding the batch lock fails retain_before and retain_before_carrying_state with a panic, never a hang. A batch is written and flushed through the log's own descriptor, shared (the Arc itself, which a dup cannot pass), so no batch opens a descriptor; on an Ordered log the batch's barrier, and the full flush a refused barrier falls back to, run in the same unlocked half with the refusing-device seam it began with, and the latch and the flush counts are applied only at its locked end (in the window the log still serves Ordered and has counted nothing). After retention replaces the file the next batch is written through the new one, and is in the file a reopen reads. Measured by examples/broker_unlocked_write_bench (reads beside a busy Strict writer; delivery with a second batch queued), not asserted. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --test suite -- unlocked_write:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib broker::tests::the_log_lock_is_held_while_staging_and_free_for_the_write && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib broker::tests::in_the_write_window_the_log_refuses_every_other_mutation && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib store::tests::a_batch_is_written_through_the_logs_own_file_and_never_a_stale_one && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib store::tests::a_fault_armed_in_the_write_window_is_the_next_batchs_rollback_included && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib store::ordered_tests::an_ordered_batch_is_flushed_in_its_unlocked_half_and_settled_at_its_end && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features retention --lib broker::tests::retention_after_a_writer_panic_fails_and_never_hangs` |
| `broker.will-fires-exactly-once` | A Will registers a record the broker publishes for a connection when it ends: persisted first as a hidden /a/will record, fired as an ordinary deduped publish, and fenced (skipped if the producer has since published a higher sequence). A dropped connection's `gone` row lands right after its live row; a later incarnation fences an earlier one's will; acks never fence it. The fence rests on a PRODUCER OBLIGATION that the broker cannot check. Request::Will and Client::will state it. In the TlaWillFencing island WillFencingInv (a will that fired was not fenced) holds for any producer, and the obligation is the hypothesis of what the fence buys: the newer incarnation's publish fences the will, every later step keeps it fenced (the high water never falls), and a fenced will has no fire step, so over the spec the will is fenced and unfired in every state once that publish has landed (newer_incarnation_publish_fences_the_will, next_keeps_a_fenced_will_fenced, obliged_spec_fences_the_will_once_landed). The obligation: a new incarnation's sequences sit above every sequence of any incarnation the producer ever presented, and the incarnation is flushed before its will is registered. astream exports the layout that meets the first half, with aterm's values: incarnation_base(inc) = inc << 32, will_seq(inc) = (inc << 32) \| 0xFFFF_FFFF, and INC_MAX = 2^31 - 1, past which the answer is None rather than a wrapped shift. will_seq(INC_MAX) registers, and the next incarnation's will, which lies in the ack half, is refused. A producer that reuses an incarnation is not fenced. Its two wills share one key, so whichever connection ends first fires its will and the other dedups: when the old, half-open connection ends first its stale goodbye lands over the live row and Last reads gone, and when the reused incarnation's connection ends first its own goodbye lands and the old will appends nothing. A will that dedups or is fenced appends nothing, so each such check first waits for the writer to have settled the will (BrokerStats::wills_settled, counted once the batch that staged it has published its head; a one-minute hang detector) and then shows a sentinel is the next record; it never waits on active_conns, which a connection leaves before it queues its will. One will and producer id per connection; hidden subjects are refused; registration reserves against the producer's subject bound, durably; under `cap` a will is authorized as the publish it becomes. Opening a dead broker's log fires each persisted will exactly once; a replica log never fires one. Connection-end firing is fire-and-forget (the next open completes it); a TCP peer that vanished without a FIN is noticed only on a connection that keeps alive (broker.request-keepalive), and otherwise not until the broker restarts; only a producer's latest will re-fires on open; a will proves a connection ended, not session liveness; on the Replicated tier registration is acked on the local commit, so the will is only as durable as the leader's log; the flush half of the producer obligation, and the counter that hands out incarnations, are the producer's and are not tested here (aterm tests its own: the incarnation lease). | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features cap --test suite -- will:: broker_core_regressions:: will_bound:: will_fence:: replica_injection::` |
| `broker.inbox-drain-ack-exactly-once` | client::drain (group subscription plus a commit connection) and client::ack (one ProcessAndProduce record carrying the ack body and the group advance, keyed `ACK_SEQ_BASE \| offset`) implement the inbox loop. Over Unix sockets: dropping a subscription before its commit re-delivers the same records (at-least-once); after the commit the next drain resumes past them; a fully committed group delivers nothing. An ack retry returns the same offset deduped, appends nothing, and moves the cursor exactly once. An ask at offset R is answered with re=R on the asker's lane; ForkSubscribe at R swaps the ask, keeps the recorded answer, and closes with a Mark. Crashes are modelled by dropping connections, not killing a process. Not asserted here: the ack-key contract, that retrying an ack written under the older bare-offset key appends a second answer, or the frame-atomic idle stop. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- inbox_drain:: broker_core_regressions::` |
| `broker.drain-idle-window-frame-atomic` | Subscription::set_read_timeout's idle stop is frame-atomic: with a timeout injected by a wrapper socket at an exact byte offset, a take cut 64 bytes into a 200 KiB record returns empty and the next take on the same subscription returns that record whole, leaving the stream at a frame boundary; the documented drain shape still delivers two records exactly once and commits. A sealed group subscription can set an idle window (an empty turn returns instead of parking) with its commit landing over the sealed wire, Subscription::get_ref reaches the underlying TcpStream, and a timeout 150 KB into the sealed stream, mid sealed-record and mid frame, resumes on both layers. set_read_timeout exists only for Unix, TCP and (with aead) sealed TCP streams; other stream types must bound the socket via get_ref. No timeout duration is measured. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --features aead --test suite -- client_idle_frame_atomic:: client_sealed_idle:: client_sealed_resume::` |
| `broker.subscription-idle-mark` | An opted-in idle subscription is not a silent one: after IdleMarks is acknowledged, a caught-up Subscribe or SubscribeGroup tail is sent a Mark{next, head} the moment it catches up and again after every idle interval with nothing written (IDLE_MARK, 10 s by default; Broker::set_idle_mark). Over Unix and TCP, for both verbs, the backlog arrives first, then two marks at the head, then a later record as a delivery; recv still returns records only. take stops on an idle mark it waited for, so a take with no idle window of its own returns what arrived (including a record published after the subscribe) instead of parking, and passes over marks already queued ahead of records (against a scripted stream). Subscription::recv_live arms a read deadline on the first mark: through a TCP relay frozen without a FIN it fails with a timeout; against a peer that never marks it arms nothing (the socket keeps no timeout), so a broker without idle marks costs no timeouts. A scripted broker that sends a Mark and a record and hangs up before recv_live re-arms on that mark (macOS refuses every socket option on the shut socket with EINVAL) is read, not an error: the record, then the close; setting the read or write bound on the hung-up socket is no error either, and a zero bound is still std's InvalidInput. Subscription::recv_live_event is recv_live event by event: it returns each Mark, its durable head included (the subscription keeps it), with the deadline armed on it, and a delivery before any mark arms nothing; a scripted broker that marks and hangs up is read, the mark then the close. Not asserted: that a plain recv on the frozen link parks, and the boundary that a subscription still delivering its backlog has no deadline until its first mark. Raw legacy Subscribe and SubscribeGroup over Unix and TCP receive no unsolicited marks and reach their configured quiet-read timeout, while new clients negotiate before the handoff and receive catch-up and idle marks before later deliveries. A scripted older broker refuses the unknown request with Error 5 and the new client still subscribes; the negotiation acknowledgement never arms recv_live. Marks are wire frames, never log records. The test interval is 50 ms; no latency is measured. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- subscription_liveness::` |
| `broker.client-typed-refusals-and-in-step` | A broker refusal reaches the client typed: a refused request returns an io::Error of kind Other carrying BrokerRefusal{code, msg} (read back with broker_refusal; Display is the broker's message unchanged), and is_permanent() reads codes 2, 3, 7 and 8 of proto's table as permanent. Asserted over real brokers: a record too large for the log is permanent (ERR_RECORD_REFUSED), a producer past its distinct-subject bound is not, and is told so by a code of its own (ERR_SUBJECT_BOUND, 10, read by is_subject_bound: its identical request succeeds on the same connection once another producer introduces the subject), an injected fsync failure is a refusal that is not, a record refused at the subject bound inside a batch whose fsync then failed is ERR_REFUSED (its resend lands, the slot it was refused for having rolled back) while the same refusal in a batch that commits keeps ERR_SUBJECT_BOUND, and a connection refused at the cap reads ERR_TOO_MANY_CONNS, not permanent, on Unix and TCP. A request that fails mid-exchange -- a read timeout with the late reply queued behind it, EOF, an undecodable or wrong reply, a page longer than asked, a failed write -- latches the client: the next request fails BrokenPipe, writes nothing, and is not a refusal; a refusal leaves the client in step, and so does a pipelined publish the broker refuses or the client cannot frame. A subscription stream cut inside a frame header is UnexpectedEof, classified at every byte exactly as read_frame classifies it. A broker that predates codes 7 and 8 sends 5 for every refusal, and one that predates 10 sends 5 for the subject bound; 5 reads as not permanent (the fail-safe reading, asserted above on the fsync refusal); no older broker binary is run here. | `targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --test suite -- client_regressions:: conn_cap:: last_value:: group_commit:: && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib client::tests::a_close_mid_frame && targo --unverified test --locked -p astream-broker --lib broker::tests::a_refusal_inside_a_batch_that_rolled_back` |
| `broker.client-owed-acks` | The client keeps its own in-step rule for pipelined publishes. It counts the acks it owes itself (Client::owed_acks; an AnyClient is a Client): send_publish and send_retire add one once the frame is written, and recv_publish_ack pays one for every whole reply it reads, an ack or the publish's refusal. While any is owed, every request that reads its own reply is refused with AcksOwed{owed} inside an io::Error of kind ResourceBusy (read back with acks_owed; not a BrokerRefusal), and nothing is written or read: hello, hello_identity, log_identity, attach_with_proof, publish, publish_pipelined, retire_subject, retire, commit, process_and_produce, ack, will, keepalive, keepalive_if_idle, idle_marks, subject_budget, fetch (a page and the head query, and oldest, which is one), last, last_page, last_walk and last_all, attach and attach_all with the cap feature, and the consuming subscribe, subscribe_group and fork_subscribe; against a scripted stream that records every byte written, not one byte is written and the two acks queued for the two owed publishes are then read by recv_publish_ack, in order, and a commit after them reads its own reply. The three consuming verbs take the client by value, so their refusal drops it: the stream is closed with the owed acks unread (and a will registered on the connection fires), which their docs and AcksOwed's say, telling a caller to read owed_acks down to zero before it subscribes. A send_retire owes its ack as a send_publish does: a commit behind it is refused with nothing written, recv_publish_ack reads the retire's ack, and the commit then reads its own reply; retire reads the one ack its own send_retire wrote and owes nothing after. Against a real broker, a commit sent behind an owed publish is refused, the publish's ack is the next frame, and after it the same commit returns its own record's offset (1, after the publish's 0), and a head query on the same connection counts exactly the publish and one commit; behind a publish the broker refuses, the commit is refused for the owed ack and recv_publish_ack returns the publish's refusal. The negative control is the same client with the guard switched off (a field only these unit tests set): the commit returns the publish's offset (0) as its own with no error, the next recv_publish_ack returns the commit's (1), and a commit behind a refused publish returns that publish's refusal as its own.
